# Russo-Ukrainian war

This article is about the ongoing war since 2014. For the escalation since 2022, see [Russo-Ukrainian war (2022–present)](70149799.md). For other wars between the two countries, see [List of wars between Russia and Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wars_between_Russia_and_Ukraine).

The **Russo-Ukrainian war** began in February 2014 and is ongoing. Following Ukraine's [Revolution of Dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity), Russia [occupied Crimea](71944314.md) and [annexed it](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Federation) from Ukraine. It then supported [Russian separatist armed groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_separatist_forces_in_Ukraine) who started a [war in the eastern Donbas region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas) against Ukraine's military. In 2018, Ukraine declared the region to be [occupied by Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-14] The first eight years of conflict also involved [naval incidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Black_Sea_incidents_involving_Russia_and_Ukraine) and [cyberwarfare](64606589.md). In February 2022, Russia launched a [full-scale invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) and began occupying more of the country, starting the [current phase of the war](70149799.md), the largest conflict in Europe since [World War II](32927.md). The war has resulted in [a refugee crisis](70199806.md) and hundreds of thousands of deaths.

In early 2014, the [Euromaidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan) protests led to the Revolution of Dignity and the flight of Ukraine's pro-Russian president [Viktor Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yanukovych). Immediately after, [unmarked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_green_men_\(Russo-Ukrainian_war\)) Russian troops occupied [Crimea](163045.md). In March 2014, Russia annexed Crimea after an [illegitimate referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Crimean_status_referendum). Meanwhile, [pro-Russian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_pro-Russian_unrest_in_Ukraine) began in parts of southeastern Ukraine. In April 2014, Russian-backed militants [seized towns and cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas#Militants_seize_towns) in Ukraine's eastern [Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donbas) region and proclaimed the [Donetsk People's Republic](42425747.md) (DPR) and the [Luhansk People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luhansk_People's_Republic) (LPR) as independent states, starting the [Donbas war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donbas_war). Russia covertly supported the separatists with its own troops, tanks, and artillery, preventing Ukraine from fully retaking the territory. The [International Criminal Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigation_in_Ukraine) (ICC) judged that the war was both a national and international armed conflict involving Russia,[^cite_note-15] and the [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights) judged that Russia controlled the DPR and LPR from 2014 onward.[^cite_note-16] In February 2015, Russia and Ukraine signed the [Minsk II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minsk_agreements#Minsk_II,_February_2015) agreements, but they were never fully implemented in the following years. The Donbas war became a static conflict likened to [trench warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trench_warfare); ceasefires were repeatedly broken, but the frontlines did not move.

Beginning in 2021, there was a massive [Russian military buildup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prelude_to_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) near Ukraine's borders, including within neighbouring [Belarus](3457.md). Russian officials repeatedly denied plans to attack Ukraine. Russia's president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) voiced [expansionist views](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_irredentism) and challenged Ukraine's [right to exist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_exist#Ukraine). He demanded that Ukraine be [barred from ever joining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine%E2%80%93NATO_relations#Russian_opposition_to_Ukrainian_NATO_membership) the [NATO](21133.md) military alliance. Ukraine had been officially a [neutral country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutral_country) when the conflict began, but because of Russia's attacks, it revived plans to join NATO.[^cite_note-17] In early 2022, Russia [recognised the DPR and LPR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_recognition_of_the_Donetsk_People's_Republic_and_the_Luhansk_People's_Republic) as independent states. While Russian troops surrounded Ukraine, [its proxies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_separatist_forces_in_Ukraine) stepped up attacks on Ukrainian forces in the Donbas.

On 24 February 2022, Putin [announced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_conducting_a_special_military_operation) a "[special military operation](70156528.md)" to "demilitarize and denazify" Ukraine, claiming Russia had no plans to occupy the country. The Russian invasion that followed was [internationally condemned](70158804.md); many countries [imposed sanctions](70323386.md) against Russia and sent [humanitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_humanitarian_aid_to_Ukraine_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War) and [military aid to Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_aid_to_Ukraine_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War). In the face of fierce resistance, Russia abandoned an [attempt to seize Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_front_of_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) in early April. In August, Ukrainian forces began liberating territories in the [north-east](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Kharkiv_counteroffensive) and [south](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Kherson_counteroffensive). In September, Russia declared the [annexation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_annexation_of_Donetsk,_Kherson,_Luhansk_and_Zaporizhzhia_oblasts) of four partially occupied provinces, which was [internationally condemned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_Resolution_ES-11/4). Since then, Russian offensives and Ukrainian counteroffensives have gained only small amounts of territory. The invasion has also led to [attacks in Russia](70539619.md) by Ukrainian and Ukrainian-backed forces, among them a cross-border [offensive into Russia's Kursk region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kursk_offensive_\(2024%E2%80%93present\)) in August 2024. Russia has repeatedly carried out deliberate and indiscriminate [attacks on civilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attacks_on_civilians_in_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) far from the frontline.[^cite_note-\:10-18][^cite_note-GallKramer2022-19][^cite_note-HRMMU_Statement_March-20] The UN Human Rights Office reported that Russia was committing severe [human rights violations in occupied Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_of_Ukraine#Violations_and_war_crimes).[^cite_note-UN_report_March_24-21] The ICC opened [an investigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_investigation_in_Ukraine) into [war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_crimes_in_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) and issued [arrest warrants for Putin and several other Russian officials](73313153.md). Russia has repeatedly refused [calls for a ceasefire](70255183.md).

## Background

Main article: [Russia–Ukraine relations](17446303.md)

### Independent Ukraine and the Orange Revolution

Further information: [Russia–NATO relations](19066574.md) and [Orange Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Revolution)



US president [Clinton](3356.md), Russian president [Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin), and Ukrainian president [Kravchuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Kravchuk) after signing the [Trilateral Statement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budapest_Memorandum) (1994). Russia and the US agreed to respect Ukraine's sovereignty in return for Ukraine giving up its nuclear weapons.

After the [dissolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) (USSR) in 1991, Ukraine and Russia maintained ties. In 1994, Ukraine signed the [Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons](22107.md) and agreed to give up the former Soviet [nuclear weapons in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_in_Ukraine).[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23] In return, Russia, the United Kingdom, and the United States agreed to uphold the [territorial integrity](31335.md) and independence of Ukraine through the [Budapest Memorandum on Security Assurances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budapest_Memorandum_on_Security_Assurances).[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25] In 1997, Russia signed the [NATO-Russia Founding Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO-Russia_Founding_Act), in which it acknowledged that "NATO has expanded and will continue to expand its political functions".[^cite_note-26] In 1999, it was also one of the signatories of the [Charter for European Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_for_European_Security), affirming the right of each state "to choose or change its security arrangements" and to join military alliances if it wishes.[^cite_note-27] Several former [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) countries joined [NATO](21133.md), partly in response to [regional security threats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_conflicts_in_territory_of_the_former_Soviet_Union) involving Russia. In 2005, Putin said that if Ukraine and other former Soviet states wanted to join NATO, "we will respect their choice, because it is their sovereign right to decide their own defense policy, and this will not worsen relations between our countries."[^cite_note-28] Putin later said [Western powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_powers) broke promises by expanding NATO eastward, even though no agreements or promises to this effect were made.[^cite_note-Wiegrefe_2022-29][^cite_note-30]



Protesters in [Independence Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maidan_Nezalezhnosti) in [Kyiv](585629.md) during the Orange Revolution, November 2004

The [2004 Ukrainian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Ukrainian_presidential_election) was controversial. During the election campaign, pro-Western opposition candidate [Viktor Yushchenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yushchenko) was [poisoned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poisoning_of_Viktor_Yushchenko) by [TCDD dioxin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TCDD_dioxin);[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32] he later accused Russia of involvement.[^cite_note-33] The more Russia-friendly candidate [Viktor Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yanukovych) was declared the winner, despite allegations of vote-rigging by election observers.[^cite_note-34] During a two-month period which became known as the [Orange Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Revolution), large peaceful protests successfully challenged the outcome, and the [Supreme Court of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Ukraine) annulled the result due to widespread [electoral fraud](382165.md). A [re-run election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Ukrainian_presidential_election_re-run) was won by Yushchenko.[^cite_note-35] The Russian government sees the Orange Revolution as one of the "[colour revolutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colour_revolution)" in former Soviet states. According to [Anthony Cordesman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Cordesman), Russian military officers viewed such "colour revolutions" as attempts by the US and European countries to undermine Russia.[^cite_note-36]

### Russo-Georgian War

Main article: [Russo-Georgian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Georgian_War)

At the [2008 Bucharest summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Bucharest_summit), Ukraine and [Georgia](48768.md) sought to join NATO, but NATO members were split. Western European countries opposed offering [Membership Action Plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Membership_Action_Plan) (MAP) to Ukraine and Georgia, fearing it would unsettle Russia.[^cite_note-37] NATO refused to offer Ukraine and Georgia MAPs but also issued a statement agreeing that "these countries will become members of NATO" at some point. Putin strongly opposed their NATO membership bids.[^cite_note-38]

[Russia invaded Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Georgian_War) in August 2008 and took control of the breakaway regions of [Abkhazia](18933375.md) and [South Ossetia](318381.md), demonstrating Russia's willingness to use military force to attain its political objectives.[^cite_note-39] Political scientist [Paul D'Anieri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_D'Anieri) says the United States "was accused of [appeasement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appeasement) and naivete" over its reaction to the invasion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED'Anieri2023136-40] The [West](21208200.md)'s weak response in 2008—and later in 2014—contributed to Russia's assessment of Western warnings against the 2022 invasion as not serious,[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-42] and, according to political scientist Samuel Ramani, encouraged further Russian aggression.[^cite_note-43]

### Euromaidan and Revolution of Dignity

Main articles: [Euromaidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan) and [Revolution of Dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity)



Protesters at a rally on [Independence Square, Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Square,_Kyiv), 29 December 2013

[Viktor Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yanukovych) won the [2010 Ukrainian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Ukrainian_presidential_election).[^cite_note-44] In early 2013, the Ukrainian parliament overwhelmingly approved of finalising a [free trade and association agreement](39410190.md) with the [European Union](9317.md) (EU).[^cite_note-45] The Kremlin pressured Ukraine to reject this agreement; Russia [imposed embargoes on Ukrainian goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_embargo_of_Ukrainian_goods) and threatened further sanctions. Kremlin adviser [Sergey Glazyev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Glazyev) warned that Russia might no longer acknowledge Ukraine's borders if the agreement was signed.[^cite_note-46]

Under pressure from Russia,[^cite_note-47] in November 2013, Ukrainian president Yanukovych suddenly withdrew from signing the agreement.[^cite_note-48] This sparked a wave of massive protests, known as the "[Euromaidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan)." The protesters opposed Russian interference, government corruption, abuse of power, and human rights violations, including new [anti-protest laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-protest_laws_in_Ukraine).[^cite_note-Euromaidan_Analyses-49][^cite_note-50]

The protests led to the [Revolution of Dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity). On 28 January 2014, [Ukraine's government resigned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Azarov_government#Fall). On 18–20 February, [more than 100 protesters were killed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maidan_casualties) in clashes with [Berkut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berkut_\(special_police_force\)) special riot police; most were shot by the Berkut.[^cite_note-OHCHR-killings-51]<sup>: 9, 21–25 </sup>[^cite_note-52] On 21 February, President Yanukovych and the opposition leaders signed [an agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agreement_on_settlement_of_political_crisis_in_Ukraine) to bring about an interim unity government, urgent constitutional changes (which needed to be signed by the president), and early elections. However, Yanukovych secretly fled the capital that evening and did not inform parliament of his whereabouts. The next day, Ukraine's parliament unanimously [voted to remove Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yanukovych#Removal_from_presidency) from office. About 73% of the parliament and members of all parties voted to remove him, including members of [his own party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_Regions) and of the pro-Russian [Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-interfax1-53][^cite_note-Aljz01-54][^cite_note-RadioFreeEurope-55][^cite_note-huffingtonpost1-56]

On 27 February, an [interim government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Yatsenyuk_government) was established, and early presidential elections were scheduled. The following day, Yanukovych resurfaced in Russia and declared that he remained the president of Ukraine. Some political leaders in the mainly Russian-speaking [eastern](13065940.md) regions declared continuing loyalty to Yanukovych.[^cite_note-Aljz01-54][^cite_note-57]

### Pro-Russian protests

Main article: [2014 pro-Russian unrest in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_pro-Russian_unrest_in_Ukraine)



Pro-Russian protest at [Lenin Square, Donetsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenin_Square,_Donetsk), 6 April 2014, with flags of Russia, the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black-yellow-white_flag_of_the_Russian_Empire), and the [Eurasianist Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia_Party).

From late February 2014, demonstrations by pro-Russian, separatist, and counter-revolutionary groups took place in several cities in [eastern](13065940.md) and [southern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Ukraine) Ukraine.[^cite_note-\:1-58] Most of the protests were in the [Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donbas) region, made up of [Donetsk Oblast](494889.md) and [Luhansk Oblast](494904.md). In the last census, the population of the Donbas was about 58% [ethnic Ukrainian](145144.md) and 38% [ethnic Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians_in_Ukraine) (Donetsk was 57% Ukrainian and 38% Russian, while Luhansk was 58% Ukrainian and 39% Russian).[^cite_note-59] A national survey held in March–April 2014 found that 58% of respondents in the Donbas wanted to remain part of a unitary or federal Ukraine, while 31% wanted the region to separate from Ukraine.[^cite_note-60] There were smaller protests in [Kharkiv](2788558.md) and [Odesa](55545.md).

The first protests were largely native expressions of discontent with the new Ukrainian government.[^cite_note-\:1-58][^cite_note-2014Lessons-61] On 23 February 2014, Ukraine's parliament adopted a [bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_\(law\)) to revoke the [status of Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_policy_in_Ukraine) as an official state language. The bill was not [enacted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enactment_of_a_bill), but the proposal caused anger among some [Russian speakers in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language_in_Ukraine).[^cite_note-62] In the last census, 75% of people in Donetsk Oblast and 69% of people in Luhansk Oblast had Russian as their mother tongue, although most Ukrainian citizens spoke both Ukrainian and Russian.[^cite_note-63] These regions mostly consumed Russia-based media, which promoted the narrative that Ukraine's new government was an illegitimate "fascist junta" and that ethnic Russians were in imminent danger.[^cite_note-2014Lessons-61]

Russia used the protests to launch a campaign of [political warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_warfare), [information warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_information_war_against_Ukraine), and [irregular warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irregular_warfare) against Ukraine.[^cite_note-karb2-64] [Leaked e-mails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surkov_leaks) and [telephone calls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glazyev_tapes) later revealed that the Russian state had funded the separatists and had organised separatist protests, mainly through Kremlin advisers [Vladislav Surkov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladislav_Surkov) and [Sergey Glazyev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Glazyev).[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67] Ukrainian authorities arrested local separatist leaders in early March. Those leaders were replaced by men with ties to the Russian security services and Russian business interests.[^cite_note-2014Lessons-61]

On 6 April 2014, hundreds of masked men stormed and seized weapons from the [Security Service](709222.md) buildings in the cities of [Donetsk](562951.md) and [Luhansk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luhansk).[^cite_note-ArelDriscoll2023-68]<sup>: 138–140 </sup> Protesters then [stormed and occupied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_Donetsk_\(2014\)) the Donetsk regional government headquarters, raising the Russian flag and demanding a referendum on joining Russia.[^cite_note-BBC100a6414-69] The next day, the activists held a meeting in the building and proclaimed the "[Donetsk People's Republic](42425747.md)" an independent state.[^cite_note-ArelDriscoll2023-68]<sup>: 138–140 </sup>[^cite_note-70] On 29 April, armed pro-Russian activists stormed and occupied the Luhansk regional government headquarters, proclaiming the "[Luhansk People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luhansk_People's_Republic)."[^cite_note-71]

## Causes

According to political scientist [Paul D'Anieri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_D'Anieri), Russia had the following four causes to start its war with Ukraine in 2014, as well as for the [2022 war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine):[^cite_note-\:8-72]

- Russia's desire to regain control of Ukraine and turn it into a [puppet regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puppet_regime).

- Russia's conception of itself as a great power entitled to a [sphere of influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphere_of_influence) over the former Soviet Republics.

- The security dilemma in Europe, with Russia perceiving the [expansion of NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansion_of_NATO) as a threat and other East European states desiring guarantees against Russian expansionism.

- [Democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy) Ukraine being perceived by Russia as a political threat to the [authoritarian regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putinism) in Russia.

## History

The [Russian occupation of Crimea](71944314.md) is usually deemed to be the start of the Russo-Ukrainian war. This was followed by the [War in Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas) (2014–2022) and then the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), which started the [current phase of the war](70149799.md).

### Historiography



Movements of the Russian military to Ukraine's borders and into Crimea in February–March 2014

Different start dates for the conflict have been identified. According to a number of scholars,[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-Plokhy_xxi-74][^cite_note-75] the war began with the February 2014 [Russian occupation of Crimea](71944314.md), in particular the 27 February [storming of the Crimean parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_the_Crimean_Parliament).[^cite_note-auto5-76][^cite_note-77][^cite_note-ArelDriscoll2023-68]<sup>: 1 </sup> The Ukrainian government declared 26 February the "[Day of Resistance to the Occupation of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Day_of_Resistance_to_Occupation_of_Crimea_and_Sevastopol)," as on that day in 2014 a Crimean Tatar rally was held to oppose Russian interference.[^cite_note-Voice_of_Ukraine_2025-78] Some, including political scientist [Andreas Umland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Umland), prefer 20 February – the day Russia supposedly issued orders to prepare for the invasion of Crimea, which is also the date engraved on the Russian [Crimea campaign medal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medal_%22For_the_Return_of_Crimea%22).[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80] Dutch Professor of Military History, Floribert Baudet, asserted that "Russia's war against Ukraine did not start in 2014 with the annexation of Crimea" because Russia had been subverting Ukrainian sovereignty long before this.[^cite_note-Rothman,_Pepperkamp_&_Rietjens_33-81]

Analysts also differ over the nature of the conflict. Paul D'Anieri describes "a limited war in 2014, and then \[a] much less limited war in 2022."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED'Anieri2023332-82] In contrast, Ukrainian-American historian [Serhii Plokhy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serhii_Plokhy) said in 2023 of the then-ongoing war that "I decline the temptation to identify the date of February 24, 2022, as its beginning, no matter the shock and drama of the all-out Russian assault on Ukraine, for the simple reason that the war began eight years earlier, on February 27, 2014."[^cite_note-Plokhy_xxi-74]

### Russian annexation of Crimea (2014)

Main article: [2014 Russian annexation of Crimea](42228673.md)

Further information: [Timeline of the Russian annexation of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Russian_annexation_of_Crimea)



Russian troops occupying the Crimean parliament building, 1 March 2014

On 27 February 2014, [Russian soldiers without insignia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_green_men_\(Russo-Ukrainian_War\)) began to occupy Crimea.[^cite_note-CrimeanInvasion-83][^cite_note-auto2-84][^cite_note-yahoo-85] At first, Russia denied that the soldiers were theirs, instead claiming they were local "self-defense" units. Later, Putin admitted that they were Russian special forces and said that he decided to "return" Crimea to Russia when the [revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity) happened.[^cite_note-86] Russia took advantage of the uncertainty in Ukraine immediately after the ousting of Yanukovych.[^cite_note-2014Lessons-61]

&#x20;The unmarked Russian soldiers seized the Crimean parliament and government buildings in [Simferopol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simferopol), as well as setting up checkpoints to restrict movement and cut off the Crimean peninsula from the rest of Ukraine.[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88] While the armed men occupied Crimea's parliament, it dismissed the [Crimean government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ministers_of_Crimea) and installed a pro-Russian government under [Sergey Aksyonov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Aksyonov), whose party won only 4% of votes in the last election.[^cite_note-gulftimes_strelkov-89] Historian [Andrew Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Wilson_\(historian\)) and journalist [Luke Harding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luke_Harding) called this the "Crimean [coup](25914154.md)."[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91] The parliament then announced a referendum on Crimea's status. No journalists had been allowed inside, and some MPs said votes had been cast for them even though they were not present.[^cite_note-92] Russian rebel commander [Igor 'Strelkov' Girkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Girkin) later admitted:

> Unfortunately, I saw no support from public authorities in Simferopol ... Rebels assembled lawmakers to corral them into the hall so that they could vote. I was one of the commanders of those rebels. I saw that from the inside.[^cite_note-gulftimes_strelkov-89]

On 1 March, the [Federation Council of Russia](1268578.md) approved the use of armed force in Ukraine.[^cite_note-93] Ukraine's prime minister, [Arseniy Yatsenyuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arseniy_Yatsenyuk), said that Russian military intervention would be the beginning of war,[^cite_note-forceAuth-94] and Ukraine's representative told the UN Security Council that Russia was committing "an [act of aggression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_aggression) against the state of Ukraine".[^cite_note-95] Unmarked Russian special forces occupied airports and communications centres,[^cite_note-forceAuth-94] and blockaded Ukrainian military bases, such as [the Southern Naval Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_Southern_Naval_Base). Russian [cyberattacks](64606589.md) shut down websites of the Ukrainian government, news media, and social media. Cyberattacks also enabled Russian access to the mobile phones of Ukrainian officials and members of parliament, further disrupting communications.[^cite_note-jw-96]

[The referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Crimean_status_referendum) was held under Russian occupation on 16 March 2014. According to the Russian-installed authorities, the result was in favour of joining Russia. It annexed Crimea on 18 March 2014. It was the first time since World War II that a European country had annexed part of another.[^cite_note-97] Following this, Russian forces seized Ukrainian military bases in Crimea and captured their personnel. On 24 March, Ukraine ordered its remaining troops to withdraw.[^cite_note-98] On 15 April, Ukraine's parliament declared Crimea [temporarily occupied by Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temporarily_occupied_territories_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-apr15-99] Russia militarized the peninsula and made nuclear threats.[^cite_note-100] In his [Crimean speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_speech_of_Vladimir_Putin), Putin said Crimea had always been Russian and should never have become part of an independent Ukraine. He claimed that Russia did not want to take any other regions of Ukraine.[^cite_note-101]

When Russia invaded Crimea, Ukraine was a [neutral country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutral_country) under its Constitution, and Ukraine's post-revolutionary government was not seeking NATO membership.[^cite_note-NNUIU29314-102][^cite_note-103] In response to Russia's annexation and further attacks on Ukraine, some NATO members agreed to begin training the Ukrainian army.[^cite_note-104]

### War in the Donbas (2014–2021)

Main article: [War in Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the war in Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_war_in_Donbas).



Russian-aligned troops occupying Sloviansk city administration building, 14 April 2014

In April 2014, the [anti-government protests in the Donbas](#Pro-Russian_protests) developed into armed conflict between [Russian-backed separatists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_separatist_forces_in_Ukraine) and Ukraine. Russian citizens with links to its security forces had taken control of the separatist movement by this stage. Russia deployed 30,000–40,000 troops near Ukraine's eastern border in early April.[^cite_note-Buildup2014-105][^cite_note-2014Lessons-61] This military buildup was used to threaten Ukraine and hinder its response to the pro-Russian separatists, causing the Ukrainian military to divert its forces to the border instead of the Donbas. Russia began a "[hybrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_warfare)" conflict against Ukraine, involving disinformation, irregular fighters, regular Russian troops, and conventional military support.[^cite_note-2014Lessons-61][^cite_note-Fedorov-106][^cite_note-karb2-64]

#### First months of the war

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the war in Donbas (2014)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_war_in_Donbas_\(2014\)).



Russian commander Igor 'Strelkov' Girkin, who admitted sparking the Donbas war in April 2014

&#x20;On 12 April 2014, a heavily armed paramilitary unit crossed the border from Russia into Ukraine and took over [Sloviansk](2533448.md) and [Kramatorsk](2864798.md) in Donetsk Oblast.[^cite_note-Wynnyckyj_151-107][^cite_note-ArelDriscoll2023-68]<sup>: 147–151 </sup> They raided the police and [Security Service](709222.md) (SBU) buildings, seizing a large amount of weapons, which were given to local pro-Russian separatists. The unit of more than 50 militants, sent from occupied Crimea, [wore no insignia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_green_men_\(Russo-Ukrainian_War\)). They included fighters from Russia and Ukraine,[^cite_note-ArelDriscoll2023-68]<sup>: 147–151 </sup>[^cite_note-Twickel-108] commanded by former [GRU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spetsnaz_GRU) colonel [Igor 'Strelkov' Girkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Girkin).[^cite_note-ArelDriscoll2023-68]<sup>: 147–151 </sup> In an interview for Russian ultranationalist newspaper *[Zavtra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zavtra)*, he said that this action sparked the war in eastern Ukraine:

> I'm the one who pulled the trigger of this war. If our unit hadn't crossed the border, everything would have fizzled out, like in Kharkiv or Odesa.[^cite_note-\:11-109][^cite_note-Reuters_Strelkov-110][^cite_note-wil2399-111]

The first conflict deaths in the Donbas occurred on 13 April, when the Russian paramilitaries fired on Ukrainian SBU officers outside Sloviansk. Ukrainian officer Gennady Bilichenko[^cite_note-112] was killed and several wounded, while at least one Russian militant was killed by return fire.[^cite_note-Twickel-108][^cite_note-113] In response, on 15 April, the interim Ukrainian government launched an "[Anti-Terrorist Operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-terrorist_Operation_Zone)" (ATO) in the Donbas. However, Ukrainian forces were poorly prepared, and the operation soon stalled.[^cite_note-IrregularArmy-114] Russian separatist commander Strelkov said that Ukrainian forces were "extremely cautious" at first, as they did not know how Russia would respond.[^cite_note-Reuters_Strelkov-110] Due to worsening unrest in Donbas and the Russian military buildup near the border, Ukraine's armed forces reinstated conscription and were put on full alert for an invasion.[^cite_note-conscription-115]



Ukrainian [Donbas Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donbas_Battalion) soldiers in the war zone, August 2014

Pro-Russian forces carried out their first civilian killings in the Donbas on 17 April.[^cite_note-116] They kidnapped local councillor [Volodymyr Rybak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volodymyr_Ivanovych_Rybak) after he tried to re-raise the Ukrainian flag in [Horlivka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horlivka). His body was dumped in a river along with those of [Maidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan) activists Yuri Popravko and Yuri Diakovskyi – all had been tortured and mutilated.[^cite_note-HRW_April_2014-117][^cite_note-OHCHR-killings-51]<sup>: 33–34 </sup>[^cite_note-118] Russian paramilitaries kidnapped more than two dozen people in the first month of the war,[^cite_note-HRW_April_2014-117] including eight [OSCE observers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OSCE_Special_Monitoring_Mission_to_Ukraine)[^cite_note-119] and journalists such as [Simon Ostrovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Ostrovsky).[^cite_note-HRW_April_2014-117]

The separatists were supported with weaponry, artillery, armoured vehicles and volunteers from Russia, including [Chechen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chechens) and [Cossack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossacks) fighters.[^cite_note-2014Lessons-61][^cite_note-jamestown-20140815-120][^cite_note-REUeuada-121][^cite_note-122] Putin gave legitimacy to the separatists when he described the Donbas as part of the historical imperial territory of "[New Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Russia)" (*Novorossiya*) and suggested it should never have become part of Ukraine.[^cite_note-\:13-123] The separatists then began attempting to create a new entity called "[Novorossiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_State_of_Novorossiya)".[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125]

The [First Battle of Donetsk Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Battle_of_Donetsk_Airport) was the first between Ukrainian and separatist forces that involved large numbers of Russian "volunteers".[^cite_note-2014Lessons-61][^cite_note-LoshkariovSushentsov2016-126]<sup>: 15 </sup>

[Petro Poroshenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petro_Poroshenko) won the [Ukrainian presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Ukrainian_presidential_election) in May. The separatist groups held [disputed referendums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Donetsk_status_referendum) that month,[^cite_note-referendum-rebels-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] which were not recognised by Ukraine or any other UN member state.[^cite_note-referendum-rebels-127]



[Wall of Remembrance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wall_of_Remembrance_of_the_Fallen_for_Ukraine) of Ukrainian soldiers killed in the Russo-Ukrainian War

On 5 July 2014, Ukrainian forces [re-took Sloviansk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Sloviansk) and the pro-Russian forces retreated to [Donetsk](562951.md) city. Russian commander Igor 'Strelkov' Girkin said that Donetsk was still relatively peaceful until then. He admitted responsibility for the shelling of the city by Ukrainian forces.[^cite_note-Reuters_Strelkov-110]

On 17 July 2014, Russian-controlled forces killed 298 civilians when they shot down a passenger aircraft, [Malaysia Airlines Flight 17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia_Airlines_Flight_17), as it was flying over eastern Ukraine.[^cite_note-130] Investigations and the recovery of bodies began in the conflict zone as fighting continued.[^cite_note-APBodies-131][^cite_note-SMHBodies-132][^cite_note-GuardianBodies-133]

By the end of July, Ukrainian forces were pushing into cities, isolating Donetsk and attempting to [restore control of the border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fights_on_the_Ukrainian%E2%80%93Russian_border_\(2014\)). By 28 July, the strategic heights of [Savur-Mohyla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savur-Mohyla) were under Ukrainian control, along with the city of [Debaltseve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debaltseve), an important railroad hub.[^cite_note-IFheights-134] These Ukrainian successes threatened the existence of the DPR and LPR statelets, prompting [Russian cross-border shelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_cross-border_artillery_shelling_of_Ukraine_\(2014\)) targeting Ukrainian troops on their own soil, from mid-July onwards.[^cite_note-135]

#### August 2014 Russian invasion

See also: [Battle of Ilovaisk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ilovaisk) and [2014 Russian cross-border shelling of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Russian_cross-border_shelling_of_Ukraine)



June–August 2014 progression map

By August 2014, Ukrainian forces had regained much of the territory seized by the separatists.[^cite_note-2014Lessons-61] Igor Girkin ('Strelkov') urged direct Russian military intervention and said that the combat inexperience of his irregular forces, along with recruitment difficulties amongst the local population, had caused the setbacks. He stated, "Losing this war on the territory that President Vladimir Putin personally named New Russia would threaten the Kremlin's power and, personally, the power of the president".[^cite_note-The_Daily_Beast-136] Strelkov said that in early August, Russian soldiers, supposedly on "vacation" from the army, began to arrive in Donbas.[^cite_note-\:11-109] According to Nikolai Mitrokhin's estimates, by mid-August 2014 during the [Battle of Ilovaisk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ilovaisk), between 20,000 and 25,000 troops were fighting in the Donbas on the separatist side, and only 40–45% were "locals."[^cite_note-wil2399-111]

Russia dispatched what it called a "humanitarian convoy" of trucks across the border on 22 August 2014. Ukraine's security service called this a "direct invasion" and said the trucks were being used to move weapons and bring the bodies of Russian soldiers out of Ukraine.[^cite_note-137]

Russia then began a more direct invasion of the Donbas.[^cite_note-2014Lessons-61][^cite_note-138] On 24 August 2014, [Amvrosiivka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amvrosiivka) was occupied by Russian paratroopers,[^cite_note-139] supported by 250 armoured vehicles and artillery pieces.[^cite_note-140] On 25 August, a column of Russian military vehicles was reported to have crossed into Ukraine near [Novoazovsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novoazovsk) on the [Azov Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azov_sea) coast. It appeared headed towards Ukrainian-held [Mariupol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariupol),[^cite_note-Russian_Column_enters_southeast-141][^cite_note-Column_from_Russia-142][^cite_note-npr-143][^cite_note-CNBCx1-144][^cite_note-145] in an area that had not seen pro-Russian presence for weeks.[^cite_note-146] The following day, the Russian Defence Ministry said these soldiers had crossed the border "by accident".[^cite_note-BBC26AUGY2-147][^cite_note-148][^cite_note-\:13-123] Russian troops captured Novoazovsk[^cite_note-WPTowns-149] and began deporting Ukrainians who did not have an address registered within the city. Pro-Ukrainian anti-war protests took place in Mariupol.[^cite_note-wiadomosci.gazeta.pl-150][^cite_note-151] The [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council) called an emergency meeting.[^cite_note-usatoday0x01-152]



Russian-backed forces attacking Iloviask, 23 August 2014

The [Pskov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pskov)-based [76th Guards Air Assault Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/76th_Guards_Air_Assault_Division) of the [Russian Airborne Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Airborne_Forces) allegedly entered Ukrainian territory in August and engaged in a skirmish near [Luhansk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luhansk),[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] suffering 70–80 dead.[^cite_note-155] The Ukrainian Defence Ministry said that they had seized two of the unit's armoured vehicles near Luhansk and reported destroying another three tanks and two armoured vehicles in other regions.[^cite_note-156]

The [speaker of Russia's upper house of parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valentina_Matviyenko) and Russian state television channels acknowledged that Russian soldiers were in Ukraine but referred to them as "volunteers" fighting for the "[Russian world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_world_\(concept\))."[^cite_note-funerals-157][^cite_note-158] A reporter for *[Novaya Gazeta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novaya_Gazeta)*, an opposition newspaper in Russia, stated that the Russian military leadership paid soldiers to resign their commissions and fight in Ukraine in the early summer of 2014 and then began ordering soldiers into Ukraine.[^cite_note-159] Russian opposition MP [Lev Shlosberg](49037247.md) made similar statements, although he said they were "regular Russian troops" disguised as units of the DPR and LPR.[^cite_note-ShlosbergSoldiers-160] After Ukraine captured ten Russian soldiers in Eastern Ukraine, a Russian defence ministry source stated they were in Ukraine "by accident" while patrolling a section of the Russian-Ukrainian border.[^cite_note-v435-161]

#### Minsk agreement and continued fighting

Main articles: [Minsk agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minsk_agreements), [Battle of Mariupol (September 2014)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Mariupol_\(September_2014\)), and [Second Battle of Donetsk Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Battle_of_Donetsk_Airport)



A map of the line of control and buffer zone established by the Minsk Protocol on 5 September 2014

On 3 September 2014, Poroshenko said he and Putin had reached a "permanent ceasefire" agreement.[^cite_note-interfax-162] Russia denied this, denying that it was a party to the conflict, adding that "they only discussed how to settle the conflict."[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-kyivpost-164] Poroshenko then recanted.[^cite_note-nytimes-165][^cite_note-166] On 5 September, Russia's Permanent OSCE Representative [Andrey Kelin](62277905.md) said that it was natural that pro-Russian separatists "are going to liberate" [Mariupol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariupol). Ukrainian forces stated that Russian intelligence groups had been spotted in the area. Kelin said, 'There might be volunteers over there.'[^cite_note-167] On 4 September 2014, a NATO officer said that several thousand regular Russian forces were operating in Ukraine.[^cite_note-168]

On 5 September 2014, the [Minsk Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minsk_Protocol) ceasefire agreement drew a line of demarcation between Ukraine and separatist-controlled portions of Donetsk and Luhansk Oblasts.



Ruined control tower during the [Second Battle of Donetsk Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Battle_of_Donetsk_Airport) in December 2014

Despite the Minsk ceasefire, sporadic clashes continued around [Donetsk International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donetsk_International_Airport), the last part of Donetsk city held by Ukrainian troops. On 28 September, Russian-backed forces began an [offensive to capture the airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Battle_of_Donetsk_Airport). Ukrainian troops were besieged in the terminal and control tower. They were given the nickname "[Cyborgs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyborgs_\(Donetsk_airport\))" as they withstood repeated Russian attacks in grim battlefield conditions.[^cite_note-169] The siege lasted throughout winter, and most of the airport was destroyed by shelling. Eventually, on 21 January 2015, rebels took the airport with help from Russian special forces.[^cite_note-170]

On 7 and 12 November, NATO officials reconfirmed the Russian presence, citing 32 tanks, 16 [howitzer](14392.md) cannons, and 30 trucks of troops entering the country.[^cite_note-171] NATO said it had seen an increase in Russian tanks, artillery pieces, and other heavy military equipment in Ukraine and renewed its call for Moscow to withdraw its forces.[^cite_note-uatoday.tv-172] The [Chicago Council on Global Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Council_on_Global_Affairs) stated that Russian separatists enjoyed technical advantages over the Ukrainian army since the large inflow of advanced military systems in mid-2014: effective anti-aircraft weapons ("[Buk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buk_missile_system)" and MANPADS) suppressed Ukrainian air strikes, Russian drones provided intelligence, and the Russian secure communications system disrupted Ukrainian communications intelligence. The Russian side employed [electronic warfare](82272.md) systems that Ukraine lacked. Similar conclusions about the technical advantage of the Russian separatists were voiced by the Conflict Studies Research Centre.[^cite_note-173]

Ukraine declared the Russian-backed separatist republics to be [terrorist](30636.md) organisations.[^cite_note-174]

#### Minsk II agreement and battle of Debaltseve

Further information: [Minsk II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minsk_II), [Volnovakha bus attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volnovakha_bus_attack), [January 2015 Mariupol rocket attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2015_Mariupol_rocket_attack), and [February 2015 Kramatorsk rocket attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_2015_Kramatorsk_rocket_attack)

In January 2015, [Donetsk](562951.md), [Luhansk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luhansk), and [Mariupol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariupol) represented the three battle fronts.[^cite_note-175] Poroshenko described a dangerous escalation on 21 January amid reports of more than 2,000 additional Russian troops, 200 tanks, and armed personnel carriers crossing the border. He abbreviated his visit to the [World Economic Forum](172185.md) because of his concerns.[^cite_note-176]



Damage from a Russian [rocket attack on Mariupol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2015_Mariupol_rocket_attack), January 2015

A new package of measures to end the conflict, known as [Minsk II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minsk_II), was agreed on 12 February 2015, with a ceasefire due to begin on 15 February.[^cite_note-177]

Despite the ceasefire, Russian-backed forces launched an [offensive on Debaltseve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Debaltseve), a strategic town and [salient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salient_\(military\)) held by Ukrainian forces. By 18 February 2015, the 8,000 Ukrainian troops were close to surrounded and had been forced to retreat from Debaltseve under relentless fire, suffering heavy casualties. Russian-backed rebel leader [Alexander Zakharchenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Zakharchenko) had said his forces would not observe the ceasefire in Debaltseve, claiming it rightfully belonged to them.[^cite_note-178] Ukraine, the EU, and the US accused Russia and its proxies of breaking the ceasefire and said that the Russian military itself took part in the offensive.[^cite_note-179]

In September 2015 the [United Nations Human Rights Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Human_Rights_Office) estimated that 8,000 casualties had resulted from the conflict in eastern Ukraine.[^cite_note-180]

#### Static war

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the war in Donbas (2015)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_war_in_Donbas_\(2015\)), [Timeline of the war in Donbas (2016)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_war_in_Donbas_\(2016\)), [Timeline of the war in Donbas (2017)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_war_in_Donbas_\(2017\)), [Timeline of the war in Donbas (2018)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_war_in_Donbas_\(2018\)), [Timeline of the war in Donbas (2019)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_war_in_Donbas_\(2019\)), and [Timeline of the war in Donbas (2020)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_war_in_Donbas_\(2020\)).

See also: [Kerch Strait incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerch_Strait_incident)



A pro-Russian rebel looking though a firing port near Donetsk, May 2015

After the Minsk agreements, there were few changes in territorial control, while the war settled into static [trench warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trench_warfare) around the agreed line of contact, marked by artillery duels and special forces operations. Hostilities never ceased for a substantial period of time but continued at a low level despite repeated attempts at ceasefire. Both sides began fortifying their position by building networks of [trenches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trenches), [bunkers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bunkers) and [tunnels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunnel_network).[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182] The relatively static conflict was labelled "[frozen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frozen_conflict)" by some,[^cite_note-REU21JULY2015-183] though fighting never completely stopped.[^cite_note-2014Lessons-61][^cite_note-184] Between 2014 and 2022 there were 29 [ceasefires](592591.md), each agreed to remain in force indefinitely. However, none of them lasted more than two weeks.[^cite_note-7265424Donbass-185]



[OSCE observers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OSCE_Special_Monitoring_Mission_to_Ukraine) overseeing the withdrawal of Ukrainian heavy weaponry from the warzone, March 2015

US and international officials continued to report the active presence of the Russian military in eastern Ukraine, including in the Debaltseve area.[^cite_note-186] In 2015, Russian separatist forces were estimated to number around 36,000 troops (compared to 34,000 Ukrainian), of whom 8,500–10,000 were Russian soldiers. Additionally, around 1,000 [GRU](261749.md) troops were operating in the area.[^cite_note-187] Another 2015 estimate held that Ukrainian forces outnumbered Russian forces 40,000 to 20,000.[^cite_note-188] In 2017, on average, one Ukrainian soldier died in combat every three days,[^cite_note-189] with an estimated 6,000 Russian and 40,000 separatist troops in the region.[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191]



A Ukrainian soldier in a trench in the Donbas, 2015



Ukrainian president [Petro Poroshenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petro_Poroshenko) inspects Ukrainian positions at the frontline, June 2016



Buildings damaged by shellfire near Avdiivka, January 2017

&#x20;The [Battle of Avdiivka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Avdiivka_\(2017\)) erupted on 29 January 2017 and lasted for several days. It saw the biggest and deadliest clashes in over a year, with more than thirty people killed and heavy artillery barrages. It began hours after newly elected US President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) spoke by telephone with the Russian President Putin. Some in the Ukrainian government believed that Trump's election emboldened the Russian-backed rebels. [Kostiatyn Yeliseieiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kostiatyn_Yeliseieiv?action=edit\&redlink=1), deputy head of the Ukrainian presidential administration, said it was "a test from the Russian side, of the reaction of the new American administration."[^cite_note-192]



Ukrainian [T-64](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-64) tanks in the Donbas during the 'static war' phase

More than 110 Ukrainian soldiers were killed in the conflict in 2019. In May 2019, newly elected Ukrainian President [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md) took office, promising to end the war in Donbas.[^cite_note-radiofree-193] In December 2019, Ukraine and pro-Russian separatists began swapping [prisoners of war](25008.md). Around 200 prisoners were exchanged on 29 December 2019.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197] In December 2019 Ukraine and Russia agreed to implement a ceasefire. The two sides agreed to swap prisoners of war and disengage military forces in several regions. Russia and Ukraine could not agree on the issues of the withdrawal of Russian-backed troops and the elections in the separatist-held regions.[^cite_note-198]

According to Ukrainian authorities, 50 Ukrainian soldiers were killed in 2020.[^cite_note-199] Between 2019 and 2021, Russia issued over 650,000 [internal Russian passports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_passport_of_Russia) to Ukrainians.[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201] There were 27 conflict-related civilian deaths in 2019, 26 deaths in 2020, and 25 deaths in 2021, over half of them from mines and [unexploded ordnance](406804.md).[^cite_note-OHCHR-202]

### Prelude to full-scale invasion

Main article: [Prelude to the 2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prelude_to_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine)

From March to April 2021, Russia began a major military build-up near the Ukrainian border, followed by a second build-up from October 2021 onward, in both Russia and Belarus.[^cite_note-Zsymbol-203] Throughout, Russia said it was only holding [military exercises](1225714.md), and Russia's government repeatedly denied it had plans to attack Ukraine.[^cite_note-denials-204][^cite_note-205]

In early December 2021, following Russian denials, the US released intelligence of Russian invasion plans, including satellite photographs showing Russian troops and equipment near the border.[^cite_note-wp-20211203-206] The intelligence reported a Russian list of key sites and individuals to be killed or neutralised. The US released multiple reports that accurately predicted the invasion plans.[^cite_note-diditmatter-207]

In the months preceding the invasion, Russian officials accused Ukraine of inciting tensions, [Russophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russophobia), and repressing [Russian speakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_speakers_in_Ukraine). They made multiple security demands of Ukraine, NATO, and other EU countries. On 9 December 2021, Putin said that "Russophobia is a first step towards [genocide](12441.md)".[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209] Putin's claims were dismissed by the international community,[^cite_note-animating-210] and Russian claims of genocide were rejected as baseless.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-\:14-212][^cite_note-213] In a 21 February speech,[^cite_note-\:15-214] Putin questioned the legitimacy of the Ukrainian state, repeating an inaccurate claim that "Ukraine never had a tradition of genuine statehood".[^cite_note-215] He incorrectly stated that [Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin) had created Ukraine by carving a separate Soviet Republic out of what Putin said was Russian land and that [Nikita Khrushchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev) "took [Crimea](163045.md) away from Russia for some reason and gave it to Ukraine" in 1954.[^cite_note-Putin_Ukraine_statehood-216]



A US intelligence assessment map and imagery on Russian military movement nearby the Ukrainian border, as on 3 December 2021. It assessed that Russia had deployed about 70,000 military personnel mostly about 100–200 kilometres (62–124 mi) from the Ukrainian border, with an assessment this could be increased to 175,000 personnel. Published by *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*.

[^cite_note-wp-20211203-206]

During the second build-up, the Russian government demanded NATO end all activity in its Eastern European member states and ban Ukraine or any [former Soviet state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states) from ever joining NATO, among other demands.[^cite_note-Reuters_17_Dec_21-217] A treaty to prevent Ukraine from joining NATO would go against the alliance's "[open door](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criteria_and_process_of_NATO_enlargement)" policy and the right of countries to choose their own security,[^cite_note-218] although NATO had made no progress on Ukraine's requests to join.[^cite_note-219] NATO Secretary General [Jens Stoltenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jens_Stoltenberg) replied that "Russia has no say" on whether Ukraine joins and that "Russia has no right to establish a [sphere of influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphere_of_influence) to try to control their neighbors."[^cite_note-220] NATO offered to improve communication with Russia and discuss limits on missile placements and military exercises, as long as Russia withdrew troops from Ukraine's borders,[^cite_note-221] but Russia did not withdraw.

#### Escalation in the Donbas

Further information: [Timeline of the war in Donbas (2021)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_war_in_Donbas_\(2021\)) and [Timeline of the war in Donbas (2022)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_war_in_Donbas_\(2022\))

While Russian troops massed on Ukraine's borders, Russia's proxy forces launched thousands of attacks on Ukrainian troops in the Donbas.[^cite_note-222] [Observers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OSCE_Special_Monitoring_Mission_to_Ukraine) from the [Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe](22796.md) (OSCE), which also includes Ukraine and Russia, reported more than 90,000 ceasefire violations throughout 2021; the vast majority were in Russian-controlled territory.[^cite_note-223]

&#x20;Fighting in Donbas escalated significantly from 17 February 2022 onwards.[^cite_note-224] The Ukrainians and the pro-Russian separatists each accused the other of attacks.[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226] There was a sharp increase in artillery shelling by the Russian-led militants in Donbas. Ukraine and its supporters believed this to be an attempt to provoke the Ukrainian army to retaliate, to give Russia a pretext for invading.[^cite_note-227][^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229] Ukraine's president Zelenskyy said that his military would not respond to the provocations. Separatist leaders warned that Ukraine was about to launch an offensive but gave no evidence, and *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* noted that it would be "exceedingly risky" for Ukraine to assault the Donbas while Russian troops were massed on its borders.[^cite_note-Guardian_18_Feb_2022-230] On 18 February, the Donetsk and Luhansk people's republics ordered [emergency evacuations](1536572.md) of civilians,[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233] although observers noted that full evacuations would take months.[^cite_note-234] The Russian government intensified its [disinformation campaign](70210841.md), with Russian state media airing videos on a nearly hourly basis purporting to show Ukrainian forces attacking Russia. Evidence showed that Russia was staging [false flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_flag) attacks.[^cite_note-ViceDisinfo-235][^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237]

Putin's address to the nation on 21 February (English subtitles available)

On 21 February at 22\:35 (UTC+3),[^cite_note-238] Putin announced that the Russian government would [diplomatically recognise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatically_recognize) the Donetsk and Luhansk people's republics.[^cite_note-\:15-214] The same evening, Putin directed that Russian troops deploy into Donbas, in what Russia referred to as a "[peacekeeping](84617.md) mission".[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240] On 22 February, the [Federation Council](1268578.md) unanimously authorised Putin to use military force outside Russia.[^cite_note-Hodge_2022-241] In response, Zelenskyy ordered the conscription of army [reservists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reservist);[^cite_note-242] the following day, Ukraine's parliament proclaimed a 30-day nationwide [state of emergency](298608.md) and ordered the mobilisation of all reservists.[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245] Russia began to evacuate its embassy in Kyiv.[^cite_note-246]

On the night of 23 February,[^cite_note-247] Zelenskyy gave [a speech](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Zelensky's%20emotional%20appeal%20to%20Russians) in Russian in which he appealed to the citizens of Russia to prevent war.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] He rejected Russia's claims about neo-Nazis and stated that he had no intention of attacking the Donbas.[^cite_note-250]

### Full-scale Russian invasion of Ukraine (2022)

Main article: [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md)

See also: [Peace negotiations in the Russo-Ukrainian war (2022–present)](70255183.md)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the Russo-Ukrainian war (2022–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Russo-Ukrainian_war_\(2022%E2%80%93present\)).



Animated map of Russia's invasion of Ukraine through 5 December 2022 (click to play animation)

Russia began [a full-scale invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) on the morning of 24 February 2022 in [a major escalation of the war](70149799.md).[^cite_note-251] It began following Putin's [announcement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putin's_invasion_of_Ukraine_speech) of a "special military operation" to "[demilitarise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demilitarise) and [denazify](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denazification#Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine)" Ukraine.[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-Waxman_20222-253] Minutes later, missiles and [airstrikes](323167.md) hit across Ukraine, including [Kyiv](585629.md), shortly followed by a large ground invasion along multiple fronts.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-kirbyBBC-255] Zelenskyy declared [martial law](50477.md) and a [general mobilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_mobilisation) of all male Ukrainian citizens between 18 and 60, who were banned from leaving the country.[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-Interfax_2022-257]

Russian attacks were initially launched on a [northern front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_front_of_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) from [Belarus](3457.md) towards Kyiv, [a southern front](70161012.md) from [Crimea](163045.md), and [a south-eastern front](70180401.md) from [Luhansk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luhansk) and [Donetsk](562951.md) and towards [Kharkiv](2788558.md).[^cite_note-CBS-2022-03-20-258][^cite_note-CBC-2022-03-21-259] In the northern front, amidst heavy losses and strong Ukrainian resistance surrounding Kyiv, Russia's advance stalled in March, and by April its troops retreated. On 8 April, Russia placed its forces in southern and eastern Ukraine under the command of General [Aleksandr Dvornikov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandr_Dvornikov), and some units withdrawn from the north were redeployed to the Donbas.[^cite_note-hindustannewshub.com2-260] On 19 April, Russia launched a [renewed attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Donbas_\(2022\)) across a 500 kilometres (300 mi) long front extending from Kharkiv to Donetsk and Luhansk.[^cite_note-nytimes.com2-261] By 13 May, a Ukraine counter-offensive had driven back Russian forces near Kharkiv. By 20 May, [Mariupol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariupol) fell to Russian troops following a prolonged [siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Azovstal) of the [Azovstal steel works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azovstal_iron_and_steel_works).[^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263] Russian forces continued to bomb both military and civilian targets far from the frontline.[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265] The war caused the largest refugee and [humanitarian crisis](1251700.md) within Europe since the [Yugoslav Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Wars) in the 1990s;[^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267] the UN described it as the fastest-growing such crisis since World War II.[^cite_note-268] In the first week of the invasion, the UN reported over a million refugees had fled Ukraine; this subsequently rose to over 7,405,590 by 24 September, a reduction from over eight million due to some refugees' return.[^cite_note-UNHCR-Ukraine-269][^cite_note-auto4-270]

The invasion was [internationally condemned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactions_to_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) as a [war of aggression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_aggression).[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272] A [United Nations General Assembly resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_Resolution_ES-11/1) demanded a full withdrawal of Russian forces, the [International Court of Justice](14918.md) ordered Russia to suspend military operations, and the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) expelled Russia. Many countries [imposed new sanctions](70323386.md), which [affected the economies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) of Russia and the world,[^cite_note-Sanctions_slams_Russian_economy-273] and provided [humanitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_humanitarian_aid_to_Ukraine_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War) and [military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_aid_to_Ukraine_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War) aid to Ukraine.[^cite_note-\:16-274] In September 2022, Putin signed a law that would punish anyone who resists conscription with a 10-year prison sentence,[^cite_note-275] resulting in an international push to allow asylum for Russians fleeing conscription.[^cite_note-276]

### Ukrainian counteroffensives and stalemate (2022–2023)

Further information: [2022 Kharkiv counteroffensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Kharkiv_counteroffensive) and [2022 Kherson counteroffensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Kherson_counteroffensive)



Remnants of a [destroyed](70246957.md) Russian Army column on 27 February in [Bucha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bucha,_Kyiv_Oblast)

Ukrainian forces launched counteroffensives in [the south](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Ukrainian_southern_counteroffensive) in August 2022 and in [the northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Ukrainian_Kharkiv_counteroffensive) in September 2022. On 30 September, Russia [annexed four oblasts of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_Southern_and_Eastern_Ukraine) that it had partially conquered during the invasion.[^cite_note-277] This annexation was generally unrecognised and condemned by the countries of the world.[^cite_note-278] After Putin announced that he would begin conscription drawn from the 300,000 citizens with military training and potentially the pool of about 25 million Russians who could be eligible for conscription, one-way tickets out of the country nearly or completely sold out.[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280] The Ukrainian offensive in the northeast successfully recaptured the majority of [Kharkiv Oblast](494901.md) in September. In the course of the southern counteroffensive, Ukraine retook the city of [Kherson](1025861.md) in November, and Russian forces withdrew to the east bank of the Dnieper River.[^cite_note-e890-281]

As of August 2023, the total number of Russian and Ukrainian soldiers [killed or wounded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualties_of_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War) during the Russian invasion of Ukraine was nearly 500,000.[^cite_note-282] In November 2023, the UN said that more than 10,000 civilians had been killed during the Russian invasion of Ukraine, with about half the deaths in the three months prior to the report taking place far behind the front lines. The UN attributed the deaths far behind the front to Russian use of long-range missiles and explosions of abandoned ordinance.[^cite_note-283] According to a declassified US intelligence assessment, as of December 2023, Russia had lost 315,000 of the 360,000 troops that made up Russia's pre-invasion ground force, and 2,200 of the 3,500 tanks.[^cite_note-284]

### Russian campaigns and Ukrainian Kursk offensive (2023–present)

See also: [Eastern front of the Russo-Ukrainian war (2022–present) § Russian general offensive (April 2024–present)](70180401.md#Russian_general_offensive_\(April_2024%E2%80%93present\)), and [Timeline of the Russo-Ukrainian war (2022–present) § 2026 campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Russo-Ukrainian_war_\(2022%E2%80%93present\)#2026_campaigns)

Between December 2023 and May 2024, Russia was assessed to have increased its drone and missile attacks, firing harder-to-hit weapons, such as ballistic missiles. By the same measure, Ukraine's forces were seen to be low on ammunition, particularly the Patriot systems that have been "its best defense against such attacks."[^cite_note-\:2-285]

In August 2024, the Ukrainian Armed Forces [launched an incursion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_2024_Kursk_Oblast_incursion) into Russia's [Kursk Oblast](480916.md) and, as reported by the Ukrainian side, in a few days captured an area of up to 350 square kilometres.[^cite_note-286] By 19 August, Ukraine had captured hundreds of Russian soldiers during the incursion.[^cite_note-w827-287]

In late October 2024, the US said it had seen evidence that [North Korea](21255.md) had sent 3,000 soldiers to Russia for possible deployment to Ukraine. On 28 October, NATO chief [Mark Rutte](4321056.md) confirmed earlier Ukrainian intelligence that North Korean troops had been deployed to Kursk Oblast, and the Pentagon reported an increased number of 10,000 North Korean soldiers sent to train in Russia and fight in the war.[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-i587-289][^cite_note-k484-290] On 13 November, both the US and South Korea confirmed that North Korean troops had begun engaging in combat against Ukrainian forces in the Kursk region.[^cite_note-r219-291][^cite_note-b215-292][^cite_note-u951-293] On 26 April 2025, Russia officially confirmed the participation of North Korean troops, noting their "significant assistance" to the Russian military in Kursk, which on the same day Russia claimed to have retaken.[^cite_note-294] In July 2026, Zelenskyy replaced [Oleksandr Syrskyi](69446333.md) with [Mykhailo Drapatyi](78479295.md) amidst a Ukrainian military leadership crisis and protests demanding the reinstatement of [Mykhailo Fedorov](61613606.md) as Minister of Defence and the removal of Syrskyi.[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296]

## War crimes and human rights violations

See also: [Casualties of the Russo-Ukrainian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualties_of_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War), [Humanitarian situation during the war in Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanitarian_situation_during_the_war_in_Donbas), [Russian war crimes § Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_war_crimes#Ukraine), and [War crimes in the Russo-Ukrainian war (2022–present)](70167888.md)



Executed Ukrainian civilians with wrists bound, in a basement in [Bucha](70455343.md), 3 April 2022

Violations of human rights and [atrocity crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atrocity_crimes_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War) have occurred during the war. From 2014 to 2021, there were more than 3,000 civilian [casualties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualties_of_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War), with most occurring in 2014 and 2015.[^cite_note-297] The right of movement was impeded for the inhabitants of the conflict zone.[^cite_note-unhr21-298]<sup>: 1 </sup> In the first years of the conflict, both sides detained people arbitrarily. In government-held areas, that decreased after 2016, while in the separatist-held ones, that continued.[^cite_note-299] Investigations into the abuses committed by both sides made little progress.[^cite_note-unhr21-298]<sup>: 2 </sup>[^cite_note-300]



Aftermath of a Russian airstrike on Zaporizhzhia on 8 January 2025, which killed 13 people and wounded many more

[^cite_note-301]

Since the beginning of the Russian invasion of Ukraine in 2022, Russian authorities and armed forces have committed multiple war crimes in the form of deliberate attacks against civilian targets,[^cite_note-\:10-18][^cite_note-GallKramer2022-19] massacres of civilians, torture and rape of women and children,[^cite_note-nytimes.com-302][^cite_note-303] and many [indiscriminate attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiscriminate_attack)[^cite_note-m185-304][^cite_note-v572-305] in densely populated areas. After the Russian withdrawal from areas north of [Kyiv](585629.md), overwhelming evidence of war crimes by Russian forces was discovered. In particular, in the city of [Bucha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bucha,_Kyiv_Oblast), evidence emerged of a [massacre of civilians](70455343.md) perpetrated by Russian troops, including torture, mutilation, rape, looting, and deliberate killings of civilians.[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-thetimes2-307][^cite_note-308] The [UN Human Rights Monitoring Mission in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Human_Rights_Monitoring_Mission_in_Ukraine) (OHCHR) has documented the murder of at least 73 civilians—mostly men, but also women and children—in Bucha.[^cite_note-ohchr.org-309] More than 1,200 bodies of civilians were found in the Kyiv region after Russian forces withdrew, some of them summarily executed. There were reports of forced deportations of thousands of civilians, including children, to Russia, mainly from Russian-occupied Mariupol,[^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311] as well as [sexual violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_violence_in_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), including cases of rape, sexual assault, and gang rape,[^cite_note-312] and deliberate killing of Ukrainian civilians by Russian forces.[^cite_note-313] Russia has also systematically [attacked Ukrainian medical infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_strikes_on_hospitals_during_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), with the [World Health Organization](33583.md) reporting 1,422 attacks as of 21 December 2023.[^cite_note-314] Many Russian soldiers confessed to raping, looting, and torturing Ukrainian civilians and soldiers in intercepted phone calls, which were regularly published online and showcased in a 2024 documentary film, *[Intercepted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercepted_\(film\))*.[^cite_note-315]

Ukrainian forces have also been accused of committing various war crimes, including mistreatment of detainees.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317]



Civilian casualties of the [Kramatorsk railway station attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kramatorsk_railway_station_attack), a Russian missile strike that killed more than 60 people

In 2024, the UN Human Rights Office reported that Russia is committing severe [human rights violations in occupied Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_of_Ukraine#Violations_and_war_crimes), including arbitrary detentions, [enforced disappearances](686148.md), torture, crackdowns on [protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_protests_in_Russian-occupied_Ukraine) and [freedom of speech](21401843.md), enforced [Russification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russification), indoctrination of children, and suppression of Ukrainian language and culture.[^cite_note-UN_report_March_24-21] Ukrainians have been [coerced into taking Russian passports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passportization) and becoming Russian citizens. Those who refuse are denied healthcare and other rights,[^cite_note-UN_report_March_24-21] and can be imprisoned as a "foreign citizen." Ukrainian men who take Russian citizenship can be drafted to fight against the Ukrainian army.[^cite_note-318] According to [Artem Lysohor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artem_Lysohor), Ukraine's head of the Luhansk Regional Military Administration, starting from May 2024, mothers who give birth in Russian-occupied Luhansk must prove that one of the newborn's parents has Russian citizenship; otherwise they will not be allowed to leave the hospital with their child. In addition, Russian occupation officials attempt to militarise and indoctrinate Ukrainian youth by developing a new textbook in accordance with Russian educational standards and children's participation in Russian "military-patriotic games."[^cite_note-319]

In August 2024, UN official Danielle Bell claimed that 95% of Ukrainian [prisoners of war](72481055.md) had suffered from Russian torture (e.g., beating, electric shock, or being stripped naked).[^cite_note-320]

## Peace and ceasefire proposals

Main articles: [Peace negotiations in the Russo-Ukrainian war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_negotiations_in_the_Russo-Ukrainian_war) and [Peace negotiations in the Russo-Ukrainian war (2022–present)](70255183.md)

See also: [Russo-Ukrainian war (2022–present) § Peace efforts](70149799.md#Peace_efforts)

Several ceasefire and peace initiatives have been advanced by Ukraine, Russia, and third-party nations such as the United States. Early direct talks in Belarus and Turkey in spring 2022 produced drafts under which Ukraine would adopt permanent neutrality and accept limits on its armed forces in exchange for international security guarantees, a ceasefire, and Russian withdrawal from territories seized after 24 February 2022, with the status of Crimea deferred.[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-\:17-322] These talks collapsed and the dialogue further deteriorated with the [Bucha massacre](70455343.md) and subsequent Russian territorial claims.[^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324]

[Ukraine's Peace Formula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine's_Peace_Formula) called for complete Russian withdrawal to the 1991 borders, restoration of territorial integrity, security guarantees, and reparations, but was rejected by Moscow. The [Black Sea Grain Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_Grain_Initiative) of July 2022, mediated by Turkey and the United Nations, temporarily enabled Ukrainian grain exports until Russia withdrew in 2023.[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327]

In November 2025, U.S President Trump adopted a [28-point peace plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._peace_plan_for_Ukraine), interpreted as broadly pro-Russian,[^cite_note-e610-328][^cite_note-l915-329] and, according to *[The Insider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Insider_\(website\))*, at its core, a recycled Russian document substantially written by [Kirill Dmitriev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirill_Dmitriev), a Kremlin operative.[^cite_note-j465-330] Ukraine, European allies and the US then produced revised versions.
