These were rejected by the Russians.[^cite_note-331] Russia has favoured the US proposals over EU proposals since 2025.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] European counter proposals to US drafts have included reductions on military restrictions that would be imposed on Ukraine, and the deletion of a provision that would force Ukraine to enshrine in law that it will not join NATO, along with other changes to wording and specifying Ukraine will receive 'robust Security Guarantees'.[^cite_note-\:17-322][^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335] Ukraine has repeatedly called for an unconditional ceasefire, which Russia has refused.[^cite_note-guardian_apr25-336][^cite_note-axios_mar25-337][^cite_note-pbs_mar25-338] Ukraine has called for reliable security guarantees in a peace deal and has refused to cede territory as that would violate Ukraine's constitution; Russia has insisted on limits on Ukraine's military, recognition of Russia's territorial gains, and Ukraine surrendering Ukrainian territory that Russia has not militarily conquered.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342]

## Related issues

### Spillover

Further information: [2014 Vrbětice ammunition warehouse explosions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Vrb%C4%9Btice_ammunition_warehouse_explosions), [2022 missile explosion in Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_missile_explosion_in_Poland), [Sudanese civil war (2023–present)](73556699.md), and [Nuclear risk during the Russo-Ukrainian war (2022–present)](71722489.md)

On 19 September 2023, [CNN](62028.md) reported that it was "likely" that [Ukrainian](31750.md) [Special Operations Forces](55773764.md) were behind a series of drone strikes and a ground operation directed against the Wagner-backed RSF near Khartoum on 8 September.[^cite_note-Ukraine-343] [Kyrylo Budanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyrylo_Budanov), chief of the [Main Directorate of Intelligence of the Ministry of Defense of Ukraine](1406269.md), stated in an interview on 22 September that he could neither deny nor confirm the involvement of Ukraine in the conflict in Sudan,[^cite_note-344] but said that Ukraine would punish Russian war criminals anywhere in the world.[^cite_note-345]

In September and October 2023, a series of fragments were reported found in Romania, a NATO member state, which were suspected to have been the remains of a Russian drone attack near the [Romanian border with Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romania%E2%80%93Ukraine_border).[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-Rainsford-2]

In May 2026, a Russian Geran 2 drone struck a apartment block in Galați, Romania, exploding on impact. Two people were injured and around 70 evacuated as the resulting fire was put out.[^cite_note-347]

### Control of natural resources



A map of mineral resources, coal basins, oil and gas fields in Ukraine, showing a very high concentration in the east

See also: [Russia in the European energy sector](24648652.md) and [Russian theft of Ukrainian grain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_theft_of_Ukrainian_grain)

Control of natural resources was a motive for Russia's annexation of Crimea and [southeastern Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_annexation_of_Donetsk,_Kherson,_Luhansk_and_Zaporizhzhia_oblasts).[^cite_note-Kirby-348][^cite_note-Brewster-349] Ukraine holds Europe's second-largest reserves of natural gas, coal, and titanium, and some of the world's largest reserves of iron ore and uranium.[^cite_note-Kirby-348] It is also thought to hold Europe's largest supply of recoverable [rare-earth minerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rare-earth_minerals) and one of Europe's largest reserves of lithium.[^cite_note-Brewster-349] Furthermore, Ukraine is known as the "[breadbasket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breadbasket) of Europe," being one of the world's biggest suppliers of wheat, corn, and other grains.[^cite_note-Kirby-348] The total value of national raw material stocks in Ukraine is estimated at over \$26 trillion.[^cite_note-\:3-350] The value of lithium and rare earths in Ukraine is estimated at \$11.5 trillion.[^cite_note-\:4-351] Control of Ukrainian lithium and grain would give Russia a "monopoly on the world market."[^cite_note-auto3-352]

Ukraine and Europe were heavily [dependent on Russian natural gas](24648652.md) when the war began. But Ukraine was trying to move towards [energy independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_independence) by weaning itself off imports of Russian oil and gas, instead developing its own production.[^cite_note-Kirby-348][^cite_note-Brewster-349] Ukraine's gas reserves posed "a potential threat to Russia's stranglehold on that market";[^cite_note-Kirby-348] a reason for Russia's annexations may have been to prevent Ukraine from becoming a competitor and to keep Europe dependent on Russian gas.[^cite_note-Brewster-349]

When Russia seized Crimea in 2014, it also claimed an [exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone) in the [Black Sea](3386.md) around the peninsula. This zone is three times bigger than Crimea itself and holds vast natural gas and oil reserves.[^cite_note-Broad_NYT-353] Ukraine had been trying to develop oil and gas production there. In August 2012, Ukraine's government had granted a [consortium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consortium) ([Exxon Mobil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ExxonMobil), [Royal Dutch Shell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Dutch_Shell), [OMV Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OMV_Petrom), and the Ukrainian state-owned NAK Nadra Ukrainy) rights to extract oil and gas in the Ukrainian part of the Black Sea.[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-\:5-355] The Exxon-led consortium had outbid the Russian company [Lukoil](1027881.md).[^cite_note-Broad_NYT-353] In November 2013, Ukraine signed an agreement with Italian company [Eni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eni) and French company [EDF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89lectricit%C3%A9_de_France) to extract oil and gas off the Crimean coast, which was forecast to provide Ukraine with up to 3 million tonnes of oil a year.[^cite_note-356] When Russia annexed Crimea, it seized 80% of Ukraine's oil and gas deposits in the Black Sea.[^cite_note-Cohen_Forbes-357] Russia also seized billions worth of property from [Chornomornaftogaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chornomornaftogaz), the Crimean branch of Ukraine's national gas company, including offshore gas drilling platforms.[^cite_note-Broad_NYT-353] In the words of *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)* investigative reporter Ariel Cohen, "In one fell swoop, Russia ended Ukraine's offshore oil and gas operations and bolstered its own."[^cite_note-Cohen_Forbes-357]

Aside from the Black Sea, the areas with the greatest natural resource wealth are in the eastern Ukrainian provinces that Russia aimed to seize since 2022.[^cite_note-358] About 80% of [Ukraine's oil](77988708.md), [natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_in_Ukraine), and [coal fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_in_Ukraine) are found in the [Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donbas)-[Dnipro](494909.md) region.[^cite_note-Brewster-349] According to a study published in summer 2022 by the Canadian think tank SecDev, Russia took control of energy reserves, metals, and minerals worth at least \$12.4 trillion in the Donbas. In 2022, Russian General Vladimir Ovchinsky confirmed that one of the goals of the invasion was to seize Ukrainian lithium deposits.[^cite_note-\:3-350][^cite_note-\:4-351] In January 2024, the Russian occupation administration in Donetsk Oblast granted the Russian [Ministry of Ecology and Natural Resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Natural_Resources_and_Environment_\(Russia\)) a "permission" to mine lithium in the Shevchenko deposit near Kurakhovo, estimated to be worth hundreds of billions of US dollars.[^cite_note-\:3-350]

The green transformation or [energy transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_transition) in Europe is threatening Russia's usual business model: the trade in [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel). A few months before the start of the 2022 Russian invasion, the EU and Ukraine had signed a Green Deal, or a transformation program, for Ukraine, partly because the [Ukrainian economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_economy) was at the time the most energy-intensive in the world with the most ineffective and expensive thermal power generation. Furthermore, 22 of the 30 raw materials that the EU classified as strategically important are available in large quantities in Ukraine. Russia could only benefit from the energy transition in Europe if it acquired the resources and infrastructure on Ukrainian soil. Europe would then be even more dependent on Russia. If Russia were to achieve its war goals, Russia could steal and gain more than it would lose in reduced exports to Europe.[^cite_note-\:3-350][^cite_note-\:4-351]

#### Gas disputes and Nord Stream sabotage

See also: [Russia–Ukraine gas disputes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia%E2%80%93Ukraine_gas_disputes), [Nord Stream](71965880.md), and [Nord Stream 2](50335419.md)



Major Russian natural gas pipelines to Europe



&#x20; Europe [TTF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Title_Transfer_Facility) natural gas

Until 2014 Ukraine was the main transit route for [Russian natural gas](42827258.md) sold to Europe, which earned Ukraine about US\$3 billion a year in transit fees, making it the country's most lucrative export service. Following Russia's launch of the [Nord Stream pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nord_Stream_pipeline), which bypasses Ukraine, gas transit volumes steadily decreased.[^cite_note-UkNGOSW15713-359] Following the start of the Russo-Ukrainian War in February 2014, severe tensions extended to the gas sector.[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361] The subsequent outbreak of war in the Donbas region forced the suspension of a project to develop Ukraine's own [shale gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shale_gas) reserves at the [Yuzivska gas field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuzivska_gas_field), which had been planned as a way to reduce Ukrainian dependence on Russian gas imports.[^cite_note-fdg4-362] Eventually, the EU commissioner for energy [Günther Oettinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%BCnther_Oettinger) was called in to broker a deal securing supplies to Ukraine and transit to the EU.[^cite_note-363]

In 2015, Russian state media reported that Russia planned to completely abandon [gas supplies to Europe](24648652.md) through Ukraine after 2018.[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365] Russia's state-owned energy giant [Gazprom](320489.md) had already substantially reduced the volumes of gas transited across Ukraine and expressed its intention to reduce the level further by means of transit-diversification pipelines (Turkish Stream, Nord Stream, etc.).[^cite_note-366] Gazprom and Ukraine agreed to a five-year deal on Russian gas transit to Europe at the end of 2019.[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368]

In 2020, the [TurkStream](45206475.md) natural gas pipeline running from Russia to [Turkey](11125639.md) changed the regional gas flows in South-East Europe by diverting the [transit through Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_transmission_system_of_Ukraine) and the Trans Balkan Pipeline system.[^cite_note-sabah231019-369][^cite_note-370]

In May 2021, the [Biden administration](65732174.md) waived Trump's [CAATSA sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countering_America's_Adversaries_Through_Sanctions_Act) on the company behind Russia's [Nord Stream 2](50335419.md) gas pipeline to Germany.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372] Ukrainian president Zelenskyy said he was "surprised" and "disappointed" by [Joe Biden](145422.md)'s decision.[^cite_note-373] In July 2021, the U.S. urged Ukraine not to criticise a forthcoming agreement with Germany over the pipeline.[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-wsj-20210720-375]

In July 2021, Biden and German Chancellor [Angela Merkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Merkel) concluded a deal that the U.S. might trigger sanctions if Russia used Nord Stream as a "political weapon." The deal aimed to prevent Poland and Ukraine from being cut off from Russian gas supplies. Ukraine will get a \$50 million loan for green technology until 2024, and Germany will set up a billion dollar fund to promote Ukraine's transition to [green energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_energy) to compensate for the loss of the gas-transit fees. The contract for transiting Russian gas through Ukraine will be prolonged until 2034, if the Russian government agrees.[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377][^cite_note-ft-20210721-378]

In August 2021, Zelenskyy warned that the Nord Stream 2 natural gas pipeline between Russia and Germany was "a dangerous weapon, not only for Ukraine but for the whole of Europe."[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380] In September 2021, Ukraine's [Naftogaz](3614879.md) CEO [Yuriy Vitrenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuriy_Vitrenko) accused Russia of using [natural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural) gas as a "geopolitical weapon."[^cite_note-381] According to Vitrenko, a joint statement from the United States and Germany said that if the Kremlin used gas as a weapon, there would be an appropriate response. According to Vitrenko, they were awaiting the imposition of sanctions on a 100% subsidiary of Gazprom, this being the operator of Nord Stream 2.[^cite_note-382]

On 7 February 2022, at a joint conference with German Chancellor Olaf Scholz, Biden said that if Russia invades Ukraine, the US would bring an end to Nordstream 2.[^cite_note-383][^cite_note-384]

On 26 September 2022, a series of underwater explosions and consequent [gas leaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_leaks) occurred on the [Nord Stream 1](3498125.md) (NS1) and [Nord Stream 2](50335419.md) (NS2) [natural gas pipelines](51111.md).[^cite_note-sdm21-385] The investigations by Sweden and Denmark described the explosions as sabotage,[^cite_note-ait22-386][^cite_note-zo221003-387][^cite_note-\:6-388][^cite_note-\:7-389] and were closed without identifying perpetrators in February 2024.[^cite_note-sweinv-390][^cite_note-daninv-391] The German government refused to publish the preliminary results of its own investigation in July 2024.[^cite_note-392]

### Hybrid warfare

The Russo-Ukrainian conflict has also included elements of [hybrid warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_warfare) using non-traditional means. [Cyberwarfare](64606589.md) has been used by [Russia](21093198.md) in operations including successful attacks on the Ukrainian power grid [in December 2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Ukraine_power_grid_hack) and [in December 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Kyiv_cyberattack), which was the first successful cyber attack on a power grid,[^cite_note-texty.17-393] and the [Mass hacker supply-chain attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_cyberattacks_on_Ukraine) in June 2017, which the US claimed was the largest known cyber attack.[^cite_note-wh.2018-02-15-394] In retaliation, Ukrainian operations have included the [Surkov leaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surkov_leaks) in October 2016, which released 2,337 e-mails in relation to Russian plans for seizing Crimea from Ukraine and fomenting separatist unrest in Donbas.[^cite_note-\:0-395] The [Russian information war against Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_information_war_against_Ukraine) is another front in the hybrid war waged by Russia.

A Russian [fifth column](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_column) in Ukraine has also been claimed to exist among the [Party of Regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_Regions), the [Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Ukraine), the [Progressive Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Socialist_Party_of_Ukraine), and the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church).[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397]

### Russian propaganda and disinformation campaigns

Main articles: [Russian information war against Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_information_war_against_Ukraine), [Disinformation in the Russian invasion of Ukraine](70144390.md), and [Ruscism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruscism)



[Moscow rally of 18 March 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Moscow_rally), officially known in Russia as a rally "For a world without Nazism"

&#x20;The Russian state [falsely claims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_allegations_of_fascism_against_Ukraine) that Ukraine's government and society are dominated by [neo-Nazism](54361.md), invoking the history of [collaboration in German-occupied Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collaboration_in_German-occupied_Ukraine) during [World War II](32927.md).[^cite_note-Abbruzzese_2022-398][^cite_note-auto1-399][^cite_note-\:122-400][^cite_note-Waxman_20222-253] These Nazi allegations are widely rejected as untrue and part of a disinformation campaign to justify the invasion.[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403][^cite_note-auto-404] Some of the world's leading historians of Nazism and the Holocaust put out a statement rejecting the claims, which was signed by hundreds of other historians and scholars of the subject. It says:

> We strongly reject the Russian government's ... equation of the Ukrainian state with the Nazi regime to justify its unprovoked aggression. This rhetoric is factually wrong, morally repugnant and deeply offensive to the memory of millions of victims of Nazism and those who courageously fought against it.[^cite_note-The_Jewish_Journal-405]

Ukraine has a [far-right](221220.md) fringe like most countries, including the [Azov Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azov_Movement) and [Right Sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_Sector),[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-auto1-399] but analysts say that Russia's government and mainstream media greatly exaggerate its size and influence.[^cite_note-Li_Allen_Siemaszko-407][^cite_note-Abbruzzese_2022-398] Ukraine's president, [Zelenskyy](59210523.md), is Jewish; his grandfather served in the [Soviet army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) fighting against the Nazis;[^cite_note-408] and three of his ancestors were killed in [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust).[^cite_note-Li_Allen_Siemaszko-407] In an attempt to drum up support for the war among its citizens, Russian propaganda has framed it as a continuation of the Soviet Union's "[Great Patriotic War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Front_\(World_War_II\))" against Nazi Germany.[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410] Some commentators point out that Russia claims to be "denazifying" Ukraine despite Russian neo-Nazi groups (such as [Rusich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rusich_Group)) taking part in the war and despite Putin's Russia being likened to a fascist state (see [Ruscism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruscism)).[^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412][^cite_note-413]



Pro-Kremlin TV and radio host [Vladimir Solovyov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Solovyov_\(journalist\)) voiced support for his country's invasion of Ukraine.

[^cite_note-414]



Ukrainian protester with a poster portraying Russian presidents (Putin and Medvedev) as Nazis in 2014



[Z symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z_\(military_symbol\)) [flash mob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flash_mob) in [Khabarovsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khabarovsk)

Putin called Russians and Ukrainians "[one people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-Russian_nation)" and claimed there is "no historical basis" for the "idea of Ukrainian people as a nation separate from the Russians."[^cite_note-Duben-415] Putin repeatedly denied Ukraine's [right to exist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_exist), claiming that it was created by the Russian [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks) and that it never had "real statehood."[^cite_note-416] A poll conducted in April 2022 by "[Rating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rating_\(sociological_group\))" found that the vast majority (91%) of Ukrainians do not support the thesis that "Russians and Ukrainians are one people."[^cite_note-417] In 2020, [Vladislav Surkov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladislav_Surkov), who served as an adviser to Putin on Ukraine said, "There is no Ukraine. There is Ukrainianism ... it is a specific disorder of the mind."[^cite_note-418][^cite_note-419] [Dmitry Medvedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Medvedev), deputy chairman of the [Security Council of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Security_Council_of_Russia) and former Russian president, publicly wrote that "Ukraine is NOT a country, but artificially collected territories" and that [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language) "is NOT a language" but a "mongrel dialect" of Russian.[^cite_note-Karatnycky-420] In 2024, Medvedev called Ukraine part of Russia[^cite_note-421] and said the Russian Army will seize what he called the "Russian cities" of [Kyiv](585629.md) and [Odesa](55545.md).[^cite_note-422] Medvedev has also said that Ukraine should not exist in any form and that Russia will continue to wage war against any independent Ukrainian state.[^cite_note-423] Moreover, Medvedev warned that Russia would [use a nuclear weapon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_risk_during_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) if the [2023 Ukrainian counteroffensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Ukrainian_counteroffensive) succeeded.[^cite_note-424] He said Ukrainians had to choose between joining Russia or "death."[^cite_note-425]

Fake stories have been used to provoke public outrage against Ukraine. In April 2014, a Russian news channel showed a man saying he was attacked by a fascist Ukrainian gang, while another channel showed the same man claiming to be a Ukrainian funding far-right anti-Russia radicals.[^cite_note-426][^cite_note-Russia_and_the_Menace_of_Unreality-427] A third segment portrayed the man as a neo-Nazi surgeon.[^cite_note-428] In July 2014, [Channel One Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_One_Russia) broadcast a fake story about a 3-year-old Russian boy who was [allegedly crucified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucified_boy) by Ukrainian nationalists.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430][^cite_note-Russia_and_the_Menace_of_Unreality-427][^cite_note-Putin's_Pal-431] Russian state media reported mass graves full of ethnic Russians in eastern Ukraine. Amnesty International investigated these claims in 2014 and instead found isolated incidents of extrajudicial executions by both sides.[^cite_note-432] Russian state news outlets have sometimes aired stories about alleged Ukrainian atrocities using footage from other unrelated conflicts.[^cite_note-Putin's_Pal-431][^cite_note-433]

In announcing the 2022 invasion, Putin baselessly claimed that Ukraine had been [carrying out genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegations_of_genocide_in_Donbas) in the mainly Russian-speaking Donbas region for eight years.[^cite_note-\:14-212][^cite_note-434][^cite_note-auto-404] Ukraine brought [a case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine_v._Russian_Federation_\(2022\)) before the [International Court of Justice](14918.md) (ICJ) to challenge Russia's claim. The ICJ said it had not seen any evidence of genocide by Ukraine.[^cite_note-435] Altogether, about [14,300 people were killed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas#Casualties) by both sides in the [Donbas War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas). According to the [United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights](22463.md), less than a quarter of them were civilians, and at least half of those were killed by mines and [unexploded ordnance](406804.md).[^cite_note-OHCHR-202]

The Russian censorship apparatus *[Roskomnadzor](15773661.md)* ordered the country's media to use information only from Russian state sources or else face fines and blocks[^cite_note-436] and ordered media and schools to describe the war as a "special military operation."[^cite_note-437] On 4 March 2022, Putin signed into law a bill introducing [prison sentences of up to 15 years](70646883.md) for those who publish "fake news" about the Russian military and its operations,[^cite_note-438] leading some media outlets to stop reporting on Ukraine.[^cite_note-439] Russia's opposition politician [Alexei Navalny](29174356.md) said the "monstrosity of lies" in the [Russian state media](15861222.md) "is unimaginable. And, unfortunately, so is its persuasiveness for those who have no access to alternative information."[^cite_note-440] He tweeted that "warmongers" among Russian state media personalities "should be treated as war criminals. From the editors-in-chief to the talk show hosts to the news editors, \[they] should be sanctioned now and tried someday."[^cite_note-441]



[Pro-Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainophilia) rally in [Berlin](3354.md), one of the signs saying "Denazify Putin"

[The Islamic State](9087364.md) claimed responsibility for the 22 March [Crocus City Hall attack](76418995.md), a terrorist attack in a music venue in [Krasnogorsk, Moscow Oblast](2399024.md), Russia, and published a corroborating video.[^cite_note-442] Putin and the Russian security service, the [FSB](385990.md), blamed Ukraine for the attack, without evidence.[^cite_note-443] On 3 April 2024, Russia's [Defense Ministry](7875884.md) announced that "around 16,000 citizens" had signed military contracts in the last 10 days to fight as contract soldiers in the war against Ukraine, with most of them saying they were motivated to "avenge those killed" in the Crocus City Hall attack.[^cite_note-444]

[NAFO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAFO_\(group\)) (North Atlantic Fella Organization), a loose cadre of online [shitposters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shitposting) vowing to fight Russian disinformation, gained notoriety after June 2022.[^cite_note-445]

In June 2025, despite months of [peace negotiations during the Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_negotiations_in_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), Putin declared that "I consider the Russian and Ukrainian people to be [one nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-Russian_nation). In this sense, all of Ukraine is ours" and continued threatening to use nuclear weapons on Ukraine.[^cite_note-446]

### Role of the Russian Orthodox Church in Ukraine

See also: [Religion and the Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_and_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine)



The 2020 consecration ceremony of the [Main Cathedral of the Russian Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_Cathedral_of_the_Russian_Armed_Forces), which previously had a mosaic depicting the 2014 annexation of Crimea and featured Putin and Shoigu, but it was later removed

[^cite_note-447]

The [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church) (Moscow Patriarchate) and its hierarch [Patriarch Kirill of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Kirill_of_Moscow) have shown their full support of the war against Ukraine.[^cite_note-448] The Russian Orthodox Church officially deems the invasion of Ukraine to be a "[holy war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_war)."[^cite_note-holy_war-449] During the [World Russian People's Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Russian_People's_Council) in March 2024, the Russian Orthodox Church approved a document stating that this "holy war" was to defend "[Holy Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Russia)" and to protect the world from [globalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalism) and [the West](21208200.md), which it said had "fallen into [Satanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satanism)." The document further stated that all of Ukraine should come under Russia's [sphere of influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphere_of_influence) and that [Ukrainians](145144.md) and [Belarusians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belarusians) "should be recognised only as [sub-ethnic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-ethnic_group) of the [Russians](59510.md)."[^cite_note-holy_war-449] Not one of the approximately 400 Russian Orthodox Church bishops in Russia has spoken out against the war.[^cite_note-450] Patriarch Kirill also issued a prayer for victory in the war.[^cite_note-451] Russian Orthodox priests who condemned or did not support the invasion were [punished](785595.md#The_Putin_presidency).[^cite_note-452][^cite_note-453]

The role of the Russian Orthodox Church in advancing Putin's war messaging is a vivid illustration of the complex interplay between religion and politics.[^cite_note-454] A Russia expert and fellow of Germany's University of Bremen told Al Jazeera that the ROC's participation in the war means it "faces the prospect of losing its 'universal character' and clout and of reducing its borders to those of \[Russian President Vladimir] Putin's political empire."[^cite_note-455]

On 27 March 2024 the [World Russian People's Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Russian_People's_Council) took place in the [Cathedral of Christ the Saviour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_Christ_the_Saviour) in Moscow where was adopted a "Nakaz" (decree) of the council "The Present and the Future of the Russian World".[^cite_note-456] According to some experts, such as the ROC protodeacon Andrei Kurayev, it has similarities with the program articles of the [German Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Christians_\(movement\)).[^cite_note-457] The decree talks about the so-called "Special Military Operation" in Ukraine, the development of the Russian World globally and other issues.[^cite_note-458]

### Russia–NATO relations

Main article: [Russia–NATO relations](19066574.md)

Further information: [Russian opposition to Ukrainian NATO membership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_opposition_to_Ukrainian_NATO_membership) and [Russian allegations of NATO aggression in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_allegations_of_NATO_aggression_in_Ukraine)



European NATO and CSTO member states on the eve of the 2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine.&#x20;

&#x20; [CSTO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_Security_Treaty_Organization)

&#x20; [NATO](21133.md) member states in 2022

&#x20; States affected by Russian occupation

&#x20; Russian-occupied territories

The conflict has harmed relations between Russia and the [North Atlantic Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty_Organization) (NATO), a [defensive alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_defense) of European and North American states. Russia and NATO had co-operated until [Russia annexed Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Federation) and [invaded the Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Russian_invasion_of_the_Donbas) in 2014.[^cite_note-NATO_myths-459] Some analysts and politicians perceive it to be the beginning of a [Second Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Cold_War). Because of Russian military aggression, Ukraine's parliament voted to end the country's neutral status in December 2014[^cite_note-460] and later resumed its [bid for eventual NATO membership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine%E2%80%93NATO_relations).[^cite_note-461] To deter further Russian aggression, a small NATO [tripwire force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripwire_force) was [deployed in the Baltic states and Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_Enhanced_Forward_Presence) for the first time ever, at the request of those countries.[^cite_note-462] A few NATO members began training Ukraine's military of their own accord.

In his February 2022 speeches justifying the invasion of Ukraine, Putin [falsely claimed](70144390.md#Allegations_of_NATO_aggression) that NATO was building up military infrastructure in Ukraine and threatening Russia and that the Ukrainian military was under NATO control.[^cite_note-DW_NATO-463] Putin said that Ukraine's potential membership of NATO would be a threat, warning that NATO would use Ukraine to launch a surprise attack on Russia.[^cite_note-\:15-214] Political scientists [Michael McFaul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_McFaul) and Robert Person said Russia's occupation of Crimea and the Donbas since 2014 had already blocked Ukraine's NATO membership, suggesting that Putin was using NATO as an excuse.[^cite_note-Person-McFaul1-464] Russian Foreign Minister [Sergey Lavrov](541346.md) characterised the conflict as a [proxy war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war) started by NATO.[^cite_note-465][^cite_note-466]



The NATO-Russia Council meets in January 2022 to discuss the [2021–2022 Russo-Ukrainian crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prelude_to_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine)

NATO says it is not at war with Russia, but rather its members support Ukraine in "its right to self-defense, as enshrined in the [UN Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapter_VII_of_the_United_Nations_Charter)."[^cite_note-NATO_myths-459] NATO condemned Russia's 2022 invasion of Ukraine in "the strongest possible terms" and called it "the biggest security threat in a generation." It led to the deployment of additional NATO units in its eastern member states.[^cite_note-467] Former CIA director [Leon Panetta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leon_Panetta) told the ABC that the U.S. is 'without question' involved in a proxy war with Russia.[^cite_note-468] [Lawrence Freedman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Freedman) wrote that calling Ukraine a NATO "proxy" wrongly implied that "Ukrainians are only fighting because NATO put them up to it, rather than because of the more obvious reason that they have been subjected to a vicious invasion."[^cite_note-Freedman_proxy_war-469]

[Steven Pifer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Pifer) argues that Russia's own aggressive actions since 2014 have done the most to push Ukraine towards the West and NATO.[^cite_note-470] Russia's invasion led [Finland to join NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland%E2%80%93NATO_relations), doubling the length of Russia's border with NATO.[^cite_note-471] Putin said that Finland's membership was not a threat, unlike Ukraine's, "but the expansion of military infrastructure into this territory would certainly provoke our response".[^cite_note-472] An article published by the [Institute for the Study of War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_the_Study_of_War) concluded:

> Putin didn't invade Ukraine in 2022 because he feared NATO. He invaded because he believed that NATO was weak, that his efforts to regain control of Ukraine by other means had failed, and that installing a pro-Russian government in Kyiv would be safe and easy. His aim was not to defend Russia against some non-existent threat but rather to expand Russia's power, eradicate Ukraine's statehood, and destroy NATO.[^cite_note-473]

Countering claims that NATO started and is waging a proxy war against Russia, it is pointed out that NATO states only sent Ukraine military aid in response to Russian aggression. NATO states have actually been slow in sending Ukraine offensive weaponry, and they prevented Ukraine from firing those weapons into Russia.[^cite_note-474] It was not until May 2024, more than two years into the invasion, that NATO states allowed Ukraine to fire Western-supplied weapons at military targets inside Russia, and only then in self-defence.[^cite_note-475] NATO has refused Ukrainian calls to enforce a [no-fly zone over Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposed_no-fly_zone_in_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine),[^cite_note-Freedman_proxy_war-469] and the US told Ukraine to stop attacking refineries and [early-warning radars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early-warning_radar) in Russia.[^cite_note-476][^cite_note-477]

Since the invasion of Ukraine, there has been an increase in [Russian hybrid warfare](78521073.md) against NATO and countries in Europe. This hybrid warfare is aimed at destabilizing the alliance and disrupting support to Ukraine. This has included [Russian sabotage operations in Europe](80996601.md), assassination plots, [airspace violations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violations_of_non-combatant_airspaces_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_war_\(2022%E2%80%93present\)), [cyberattacks](21093198.md), [electronic warfare](82272.md), and [disinformation](70210841.md) operations.[^cite_note-478][^cite_note-479]

### Russian military bases in Crimea



[Russian cruiser Moskva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_cruiser_Moskva) (centre) at [Sevastopol Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sevastopol_Bay) in 2012

When the [Russian occupation of Crimea](71944314.md) began, Russia had roughly 12,000 military personnel from the [Black Sea Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_Fleet),[^cite_note-2014Lessons-61] in several locations in the [Crimean peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_peninsula) such as Sevastopol, [Kacha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kacha,_Sevastopol), [Hvardiiske, Simferopol Raion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hvardiiske,_Simferopol_Raion), [Sarych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarych), and others. In 2005 a dispute broke out between Russia and Ukraine over control of the Sarych Cape lighthouse near Yalta and a number of other beacons.[^cite_note-480][^cite_note-481] [The basing and transit agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_Treaty_on_the_Status_and_Conditions_of_the_Black_Sea_Fleet) with Ukraine allowed the presence there of a maximum of 25,000 Russian troops. Russia was required to respect the sovereignty of Ukraine, honour its legislation, not interfere in the internal affairs of the country, and show their "military identification cards" when crossing the international border.[^cite_note-482] Early in the conflict, the agreement's generous troop limit allowed Russia to significantly strengthen its military presence and deploy special forces and other required capabilities to conduct the operation in Crimea under the pretext of addressing security concerns.[^cite_note-2014Lessons-61]

According to the original treaty on the division of the Soviet [Black Sea Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_Fleet) signed in 1997, Russia was allowed to have its military bases in Crimea until 2017, after which it would evacuate all military units, including its portion of the Black Sea Fleet, from the [Autonomous Republic of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Republic_of_Crimea) and Sevastopol. On 21 April 2010, former Ukrainian president Viktor Yanukovych signed a new deal with Russia, known as the [Kharkiv Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharkiv_Pact), to resolve the [2009 Russia–Ukraine gas dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Russia%E2%80%93Ukraine_gas_dispute). The pact extended Russia's stay in Crimea to 2042, with an option to renew.[^cite_note-483]

### Non-state actors and mercenaries

On 4 August 2025, Ukrainian President [Volodymyr Zelensky](59210523.md) alleged that foreign mercenaries, including individuals from Pakistan and China, were fighting alongside Russian forces in northeastern Ukraine. During a visit to the frontline in the [Kharkiv](2788558.md) region, President Zelensky stated, "Our warriors in this sector are reporting the participation of mercenaries from China, Tajikistan, Uzbekistan, Pakistan, and African countries in the war. We will respond." He had previously accused Moscow of recruiting Chinese fighters for its war effort, a claim that Beijing denied.[^cite_note-484][^cite_note-485]

### Third-party arms suppliers

#### Iran

The Islamic Republic of Iran has supplied Russia with a large number of unmanned aerial vehicles, most notably the [Shahed drones](75153890.md).[^cite_note-486][^cite_note-487][^cite_note-auto6-488] By the second year of the war, Russia was domestically producing modified versions of Iran's Shahed drones.[^cite_note-auto6-488]

#### North Korea

Initial reports that the Democratic People's Republic of Korea was supplying arms and ammunition to Russia were revealed by the White House National Security Council in 2022.[^cite_note-489] Deals expanded the following year, with Russia supplying food and economic assistance in exchange for more weaponry and munitions.[^cite_note-490][^cite_note-491] [North Korea–Russia relations](11438091.md) expanded significantly throughout the war, with both countries signing a [mutual defense treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Korean%E2%80%93Russian_Treaty_on_Comprehensive_Strategic_Partnership), which saw North Korean soldiers being deployed and used to recapture Russia's Kursk Oblast.[^cite_note-492][^cite_note-493]

#### China



Putin and Chinese president [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) in Moscow in May 2025

Samuel Bendett, an adjunct senior fellow at the [Center for a New American Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_a_New_American_Security), stated that China has become a key contributor to Russia's military supply chain.[^cite_note-\:9-494]

Both Russia and Ukraine rely on Chinese exports for large parts of their drone manufacturing and supply systems.[^cite_note-495][^cite_note-496]

According to reporting by [Reuters](18998750.md) based on European security officials and documents reviewed by the agency, teams of Chinese drone engineers and technicians visited the Russian state-owned arms manufacturer [IEMZ Kupol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IEMZ_Kupol) on multiple occasions from mid‑2024 into 2025 to conduct flight tests, assembly, and technical development work on a range of unmanned aerial systems; the documents and invoices cited shipments of Chinese-made one‑way attack and surveillance drones, including models supplied by Sichuan AEE and Hunan Haotianyi, to Kupol via the sanctioned Russian intermediary TSK Vektor and described collaborative activities such as adapting Chinese flight‑control computers and engines for the domestically produced [Garpiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garpiya) series, installing anti‑jamming equipment, and conducting test flights at a Russian military range.[^cite_note-\:9-494]

### Legality and declaration of war

Further information: [Putin's invasion of Ukraine speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putin's_invasion_of_Ukraine_speech)



Territorial control in Ukraine and nearby areas of Russia as of July 2025

No formal [declaration of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_war) has been issued in the ongoing Russo-Ukrainian War. When Putin announced the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), he claimed to commence a "[special military operation](70156528.md)," sidestepping a formal declaration of war.[^cite_note-497] The statement was, however, regarded by the Ukrainian government as a declaration of war[^cite_note-498] and reported as such by many international news sources.[^cite_note-499][^cite_note-500] While the Ukrainian parliament refers to Russia as a "terrorist state" in regard to its military actions in Ukraine,[^cite_note-501] it has not issued a formal declaration of war on its behalf.

The Russian invasion of Ukraine violated [international law](8195726.md) (including the [Charter of the United Nations](88805.md)).[^cite_note-509][^cite_note-510][^cite_note-511][^cite_note-512] The invasion has also been called a [crime of aggression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_of_aggression) under [international criminal law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_criminal_law)[^cite_note-513] and under some countries' domestic [criminal codes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_code)—including those of [Ukraine](31750.md) and Russia—although procedural obstacles exist to prosecutions under these laws.[^cite_note-\:02-514][^cite_note-\:12-515]

## Reactions

Further information: [International sanctions during the Russo-Ukrainian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War) and [List of military aid to Ukraine during the Russo-Ukrainian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_aid_to_Ukraine_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War)

See also: [Second Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Cold_War)

### Reactions to the Russian annexation of Crimea

Main article: [International reactions to the Russian annexation of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reactions_to_the_Russian_annexation_of_Crimea)

#### Ukrainian response



Following Russia's annexation of Crimea, Ukraine blocked the [North Crimean Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Crimean_Canal), which provided 85% of Crimea's drinking and irrigation water.

[^cite_note-516]

Interim Ukrainian president [Oleksandr Turchynov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oleksandr_Turchynov) accused Russia of "provoking a conflict" by backing the seizure of the Crimean parliament building and other government offices on the Crimean peninsula. He compared Russia's military actions to the 2008 [Russo-Georgian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Georgian_War), when Russian troops occupied parts of the [Republic of Georgia](48768.md) and the breakaway enclaves of [Abkhazia](18933375.md) and [South Ossetia](318381.md) were established under the control of Russian-backed administrations. He called on Putin to withdraw Russian troops from Crimea and stated that Ukraine will "preserve its territory" and "defend its independence."[^cite_note-517] On 1 March, he warned, "Military intervention would be the beginning of war and the end of any relations between Ukraine and Russia."[^cite_note-518] On 1 March, acting president Oleksandr Turchynov placed the Armed Forces of Ukraine on full alert and combat readiness.[^cite_note-519]

The [Ministry of Temporarily Occupied Territories and IDPs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Temporarily_Occupied_Territories_and_IDPs) was established by the Ukrainian government on 20 April 2016 to manage occupied parts of the [Donetsk, Luhansk and Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temporarily_occupied_and_uncontrolled_territories_of_Ukraine_\(2014-present\)) regions affected by the Russian military intervention of 2014.[^cite_note-NMC20416-520]

#### NATO and United States military response

Further information: [Operation Atlantic Resolve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Atlantic_Resolve), [European Deterrence Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Deterrence_Initiative), [NATO Enhanced Forward Presence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_Enhanced_Forward_Presence), and [Russia–NATO relations](19066574.md)



A U.S. Army convoy in [Vilseck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilseck), Germany during [Operation Atlantic Resolve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Atlantic_Resolve), NATO's efforts to reassert its military presence in central and eastern Europe that began in April 2014.

On 4 March 2014, the United States pledged \$1 billion in aid to Ukraine.[^cite_note-521] Russia's actions increased tensions in nearby countries historically within its [sphere of influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphere_of_influence), particularly the [Baltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_states) and [Moldova](19260.md). All have large Russian-speaking populations, and [Russian troops are stationed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_military_presence_in_Transnistria) in the breakaway Moldovan territory of [Transnistria](376581.md).[^cite_note-Herald-522] Some devoted resources to increasing defensive capabilities,[^cite_note-NTI-523] and many requested increased support from the U.S. and the [North Atlantic Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty_Organization), which they had joined in recent years.[^cite_note-Herald-522][^cite_note-NTI-523] The conflict "reinvigorated" NATO, which had been created to face the Soviet Union but had devoted more resources to "expeditionary missions" in recent years.[^cite_note-Gearan-524]

In addition to diplomatic support in its conflict with Russia, the U.S. provided Ukraine with US\$1.5 billion in military aid during the 2010s.[^cite_note-525] In 2018 the [U.S. House of Representatives](19468510.md) passed a provision blocking any training of the [Azov Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azov_Battalion) of the [Ukrainian National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_defense_battalions_\(Ukraine\)) by American forces. In previous years, between 2014 and 2017, the U.S. House of Representatives passed amendments banning support of Azov, but due to pressure from the [Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon), the amendments were quietly lifted.[^cite_note-526][^cite_note-527][^cite_note-528]

#### Financial markets



[Euro](9472.md)/[RUB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_ruble) [exchange rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exchange_rate)



[USD](18717338.md)/Russian Ruble Exchange Rate



Russian bonds displayed [inverted yield curves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inverted_yield_curve) as the government sought to tame inflation during the Russo-Ukrainian War and [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) &#x20;

&#x20; 20 year bond

&#x20; 10 year bond

&#x20; 1 year bond

&#x20; 3 month bond

The initial reaction to the escalation of tensions in Crimea caused the Russian and European stock markets to tumble.[^cite_note-529] The intervention caused the Swiss franc to climb to a 2-year high against the dollar and a 1-year high against the euro. The Euro and the US dollar both rose, as did the Australian dollar.[^cite_note-20140303reuters-530] The Russian stock market declined by more than 10 percent, while the Russian [ruble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruble) hit all-time lows against the US dollar and the Euro.[^cite_note-WIRDIU-531][^cite_note-532][^cite_note-20140303aphosted-533] The Russian central bank hiked interest rates and intervened in the foreign exchange markets to the tune of \$12 billion to try to stabilise its currency.[^cite_note-20140303reuters-530] Prices for wheat and grain rose, with Ukraine being a major exporter of both crops.[^cite_note-534]

Later in March 2014, the reaction of the financial markets to the Crimea annexation was surprisingly mellow, with global financial markets rising immediately after the referendum held in Crimea, one explanation being that the sanctions were already priced in following the earlier Russian incursion.[^cite_note-535] Other observers considered that the positive reaction of the global financial markets on Monday 17 March 2014, after the announcement of sanctions against Russia by the EU and the US, revealed that these sanctions were too weak to hurt Russia.[^cite_note-536] In early August 2014, the German [DAX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DAX) was down by 6 percent for the year and 11 percent since June, over concerns Russia, Germany's 13th biggest trade partner, would retaliate against sanctions.[^cite_note-SanctionsGermany-537]

### Reactions to the war in Donbas

Further information: [International reactions to the war in Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reactions_to_the_war_in_Donbas)

#### Ukrainian public opinion

See also: [Putin khuylo!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putin_khuylo!)



Pro-Russian supporters in [Donetsk](562951.md), 20 December 2014

From 12 to 25 September 2014, the [International Republican Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Republican_Institute) ran a poll, excluding Russian-annexed [Crimea](163045.md), of the Ukrainian public. 89% of those polled opposed 2014 Russian military intervention in Ukraine. As broken down by region, 78% of those polled from [Eastern Ukraine](13065940.md) (including [Dnipropetrovsk Oblast](494909.md)) opposed said intervention, along with 89% in [Southern Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Ukraine), 93% in [Central Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Ukraine), and 99% in [Western Ukraine](3258511.md). As broken down by native language, 79% of Russian speakers and 95% of Ukrainian speakers opposed the intervention. 80% of those polled said the country should remain a [unitary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) country.[^cite_note-IRI14OCT-538]

On 16–22 January 2015, the Ukrainian branch of Germany's biggest market research organisation, [GfK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GfK), ran a poll of the [Crimean](163045.md) public in Russian-annexed Crimea. According to its results, "Eighty-two per cent of those polled said they fully supported Crimea's inclusion in Russia, and another 11 per cent expressed partial support. Only 4 per cent spoke out against it."[^cite_note-539][^cite_note-540][^cite_note-541]

A joint poll conducted by Levada and the [Kyiv International Institute of Sociology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyiv_International_Institute_of_Sociology) from September to October 2020 found that in the breakaway regions controlled by the [DPR](42425747.md)/[LPR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luhansk_People's_Republic), just over half of the respondents wanted to join Russia (either with or without some autonomous status), while less than one-tenth wanted independence and 12% wanted reintegration into Ukraine. It contrasted with respondents in Kyiv-controlled Donbas, where a vast majority felt the separatist regions should be returned to Ukraine.[^cite_note-542] According to results from Levada in January 2022, roughly 70% of those in the breakaway regions said their territories should become part of the Russian Federation.[^cite_note-543]

#### Russian public opinion

See also: [2014 anti-war protests in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_anti-war_protests_in_Russia)



Peace march in [Moscow](19004.md), 21 September 2014

An August 2014 survey by the [Levada Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levada_Center) reported that only 13% of those Russians polled would support the Russian government in an open war with Ukraine.[^cite_note-544] Street protests against the war in Ukraine arose in Russia. Notable protests first occurred in March[^cite_note-545][^cite_note-afp-546] and large protests occurred in September when "tens of thousands" protested the war in Ukraine with a peace march in downtown Moscow on Sunday, 21 September 2014, "under heavy police supervision."[^cite_note-wp20140921-547]

### Reactions to the 2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine

Main article: [Reactions to the 2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactions_to_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine)

#### Ukrainian public opinion

See also: [Russian warship, go fuck yourself](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_warship,_go_fuck_yourself); [*Orc* (slang)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orc_\(slang\)); [Ghost of Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghost_of_Kyiv); [Saint Javelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Javelin); [Bayraktar (song)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayraktar_\(song\)); and [Good Evening (Where Are You From?)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Evening_\(Where_Are_You_From%3F\))



[Ukrainian refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Ukrainian_refugee_crisis) in [Kraków](16815.md) protest against the war, 6 March 2022

In March 2022, a week after the Russian invasion of Ukraine, 98% of Ukrainians—including 82% of [ethnic Russians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians_in_Ukraine) living in Ukraine—said they did not believe that any part of Ukraine was rightfully part of Russia, according to [Lord Ashcroft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Ashcroft)'s polls which did not include Crimea and the separatist-controlled part of Donbas. 97% of Ukrainians said they had an unfavourable view of Russian president Vladimir Putin, with a further 94% saying they had an unfavourable view of the Russian Armed Forces.[^cite_note-548]

At the end of 2021, 75% of Ukrainians said they had a positive attitude toward ordinary Russians, while in May 2022, 82% of Ukrainians said they had a negative attitude toward ordinary Russians.[^cite_note-549]

A [Razumkov Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Razumkov_Centre) poll conducted from 19 to 25 January 2024 found that Russia was the most negatively viewed country in Ukraine, with it being viewed negatively by 95% of Ukrainian respondents. The second, third and fourth most negatively viewed countries were Belarus (87%), Iran (82%) and China (72.5%), respectively. Ukrainian respondents were most positive towards Lithuania (91%), Latvia (90.5%), the UK (90%), Germany (89%), Estonia (89%), Canada (88%) and the US (87%).[^cite_note-550][^cite_note-551]

[Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_Inc.) opinion polls on whether Ukrainians want a negotiated end to the war as soon as possible, rather than fighting until Ukraine wins, have increased from 22% at the start of the war to 69% in 2025, with it becoming the majority opinion in late 2023.[^cite_note-telegraph-20250807-552][^cite_note-news-20250807-553]

#### Russian public opinion

See also: [Anti-war protests in Russia (2022–present)](70159281.md)



&#x20;  Russia\
&#x20;   Countries on Russia's "[Unfriendly Countries List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unfriendly_Countries_List)". The list includes countries that have imposed [sanctions against Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War) for its invasion of Ukraine.

[^cite_note-554]

An April 2022 survey by the Levada Centre reported that approximately 74% of the Russians polled supported the "special military operation" in Ukraine, suggesting that Russian public opinion has shifted considerably since 2014.[^cite_note-555] According to some sources, a reason many Russians supported the "special military operation" has to do with the [propaganda](45060755.md) and disinformation.[^cite_note-556][^cite_note-557] In addition, it has been suggested that some respondents did not want to answer pollsters' questions for fear of negative consequences.[^cite_note-558][^cite_note-559] At the end of March, a poll conducted in Russia by the Levada Center concluded the following: When asked why they think the military operation is taking place, respondents said it was to protect and defend civilians, ethnic Russians or Russian speakers in Ukraine (43%), to prevent an attack on Russia (25%), to get rid of nationalists and "denazify" Ukraine (21%), and to incorporate Ukraine or the Donbas region into Russia (3%)."[^cite_note-560] According to polls, the Russian president's rating rose from 71% on the eve of the invasion to 82% in March 2023.[^cite_note-561]

The Kremlin's analysis concluded that public support for the war was broad but not deep and that most Russians would accept anything Putin would call a victory. In September 2023, the head of the [VTsIOM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Public_Opinion_Research_Center) state pollster, Valery Fyodorov, said in an interview that only 10–15% of Russians actively supported the war and that "most Russians are not demanding the conquest of Kyiv or Odesa."[^cite_note-562] On the accuracy of public opinion polls, Russian-born political activist [Vladimir Kara-Murza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Kara-Murza) said, "I see Russian pollsters say that the average response rate to polls in Russia average from five to seven per-cent; some 90% of the people refuse to say in polls, and \[for] very good reasons. It is impossible to judge the true state of public opinion in a country that imprisons you for expressing it. ...The Putin propaganda machine tries to pretend that Russian society is a monolith, that all Russians support this war, that all Russians support this regime, and this is a lie, needless to say."[^cite_note-563]

In 2023, [Oleg Orlov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oleg_Orlov), the chairman of the Board of Human Rights Center "[Memorial](570357.md)," claimed that [Russia under Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_under_Vladimir_Putin) had descended into [fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism) and that the army was committing "mass murder."[^cite_note-564][^cite_note-565]

#### United States

See also: [United States and the Russian invasion of Ukraine](70376708.md)



American president [Joe Biden](145422.md) holding a [Ukrainian refugee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_refugee_crisis_\(2022%E2%80%93present\)) at Warsaw's [Kazimierz Górski National Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazimierz_G%C3%B3rski_National_Stadium) in a meeting where he described Russian president Vladimir Putin as "butcher", 26 March 2022

[^cite_note-566]

On 28 April 2022, US President [Joe Biden](145422.md) asked [Congress](31756.md) for an additional \$33 billion to assist Ukraine, including \$20 billion to provide weapons to Ukraine.[^cite_note-567] On 5 May, Ukraine's Prime Minister [Denys Shmyhal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denys_Shmyhal) announced that Ukraine had received more than \$12 billion worth of weapons and financial aid from Western countries since the start of Russia's invasion on 24 February.[^cite_note-568] On 21 May 2022, the [United States](3434750.md) passed legislation providing \$40 billion in new military and humanitarian foreign aid to Ukraine, marking a historically large commitment of funds.[^cite_note-\:16-274][^cite_note-569] In August 2022, U.S. defence spending to counter the Russian war effort exceeded the first 5 years of war costs in [Afghanistan](737.md). The [Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Post) reported that new U.S. weapons delivered to the Ukrainian war front suggest a closer combat scenario with more casualties.[^cite_note-washingtonpost.com-570] The United States looks to build "enduring strength in Ukraine" with increased arms shipments and a record-breaking \$3 billion military aid package.[^cite_note-washingtonpost.com-570]

On 7 March 2024, American president Joe Biden, in his [2024 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_State_of_the_Union_Address), compared [Russia under Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_under_Vladimir_Putin) to [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md)'s [conquests of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German-occupied_Europe).[^cite_note-571]

Following the [second inauguration of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Donald_Trump), the [American administration](24113.md)'s support to Ukraine decreased, and in March 2025 the United States paused military aid to Ukraine after the [2025 Trump–Zelenskyy Oval Office meeting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Trump%E2%80%93Zelenskyy_Oval_Office_meeting); the aid was later resumed.[^cite_note-572] On 22 May 2025, after months of [peace negotiations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_negotiations_in_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) without a bilateral agreement between Russia and Ukraine, the United States together with other [G7](1196634.md) countries united to condemn Russia's continued brutal war against Ukraine. The G7 also declared its continued commitment to supporting Ukraine in defending the country's territorial integrity and right to exist, as well as its freedom, sovereignty, and independence, all with the aim of a just and durable peace.[^cite_note-573]

#### Russian military suppliers

See also: [Axis of Upheaval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_of_Upheaval) and [Support for Russia in the Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Support_for_Russia_in_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine)



[Kim Jong Un](5962511.md) and Putin [meeting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_North_Korea%E2%80%93Russia_summit) at [Vostochny Cosmodrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vostochny_Cosmodrome) in 2023 where Kim gave his support for Russia's "sacred fight" against the "hegemonic forces that oppose Russia"

[^cite_note-574]

After expending large amounts of heavy weapons and munitions over months, Russia received combat drones, loitering munitions, and large amounts of artillery from [Iran](14653.md), deliveries of tanks and other armoured vehicles from [Belarus](3457.md), and reportedly planned to trade for artillery ammunition from [North Korea](21255.md) and ballistic missiles from Iran.[^cite_note-575][^cite_note-576][^cite_note-577][^cite_note-578][^cite_note-579]

The U.S. has accused [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_and_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) of providing Russia with technology it needs for high-tech weapons, allegations that China has denied. The U.S. sanctioned a Chinese firm for providing satellite imagery to Russian mercenary forces fighting in Ukraine.[^cite_note-580]

In March 2023, Western nations had pressed the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) to halt [re-exports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Re-exports) of goods to Russia that had military uses, amidst allegations that the Gulf country exported 158 drones to Russia in 2022.[^cite_note-581] In May 2023, the U.S. accused South Africa of supplying arms to Russia in a covert naval operation,[^cite_note-582] allegations which have been denied by South African president [Cyril Ramaphosa](1089194.md).[^cite_note-583]

#### United Nations



United Nations secretary-general [António Guterres](471806.md) and Ukrainian president Volodymyr Zelenskyy delegations meeting in April 2022

On 25 February 2022, the [Security Council](31956.md) failed to adopt a draft resolution that would have "deplored, in the strongest terms, the Russian Federation's aggression" on Ukraine. Of the 15 member states on the Security Council, 11 were in support, while three abstained from voting. The draft resolution failed due to a Russian veto.[^cite_note-584][^cite_note-585]

Due to the deadlock, the Security Council passed a resolution to convene the [General Assembly](31957.md) for the [eleventh emergency special session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleventh_emergency_special_session_of_the_United_Nations_General_Assembly).[^cite_note-586] On 2 March 2022, the General Assembly voted to deplore "in the strongest possible terms" Russia's aggression against Ukraine by a vote of 141 to 5, with 35 [abstentions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstention).[^cite_note-generalassembly-587] The [resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_Resolution_ES-11/1) also called for the Russian Federation to "immediately cease its use of force against Ukraine" and "immediately, completely, and unconditionally withdraw all of its military forces."[^cite_note-generalassembly-587] Only Russia, Belarus, [Syria](57134800.md), North Korea, and [Eritrea](17238590.md) voted against the resolution.[^cite_note-588]

On 4 March 2022, the [UN Human Rights Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Human_Rights_Council) adopted a resolution by a vote of 32 to 2, with 13 abstentions, calling for the withdrawal of Russian troops and Russian-backed armed groups from Ukraine and humanitarian access to people in need. The resolution also established a commission to investigate alleged rights violations committed during Russia's military attack on Ukraine.[^cite_note-589]

In October 2022, the United Nations General Assembly had adopted a resolution condemning the [2022 annexation referendums in Russian-occupied Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_annexation_referendums_in_Russian-occupied_Ukraine) with 143 supporting votes, 5 opposing votes (Belarus, North Korea, [Nicaragua](21362.md), Russia, and Syria), and 35 abstentions.[^cite_note-590]

[^cite_note-Note_1-1]: &#x20;There are "some contradictions and inherent problems" regarding the date on which the occupation began.<sup>[\[587\]](#cite_note-Disunited-591)</sup> The Ukrainian Government maintains, and the [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights) agrees, that Russia controlled Crimea from 27 February 2014,<sup>[\[588\]](#cite_note-592)</sup> when unmarked Russian commandos seized its political institutions.<sup>[\[72\]](#cite_note-auto5-76)</sup> The Russian Government later made 27 February "Special Operations Forces Day".<sup>[\[80\]](#cite_note-auto2-84)</sup> In 2015, the [Ukrainian parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_parliament) officially designated 20 February 2014 as "the beginning of the [temporary occupation of Crimea and Sevastopol by Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temporarily_occupied_and_uncontrolled_territories_of_Ukraine)",<sup>[\[589\]](#cite_note-593)</sup> citing the date inscribed on the Russian [medal "For the Return of Crimea"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medal_%22For_the_Return_of_Crimea%22).<sup>[\[590\]](#cite_note-594)</sup> On that date, [Vladimir Konstantinov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Konstantinov_\(politician\)), then Chairman of the [Supreme Council of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verkhovna_Rada_of_Crimea), had said the region would be prepared to join Russia.<sup>[\[591\]](#cite_note-595)</sup> In 2018, Russian Foreign Minister Sergey Lavrov claimed that the earlier "start date" on the medal was due to a "technical misunderstanding".<sup>[\[592\]](#cite_note-596)</sup> President Putin stated in a [Russian film about the annexation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimea._The_Way_Home) that he ordered the operation to "restore" Crimea to Russia following an all-night emergency meeting on 22–23 February 2014.<sup>[\[587\]](#cite_note-Disunited-591)</sup><sup>[\[593\]](#cite_note-Yahoo_News-597)</sup>

He was a Ukrainian officer and part of a group that had strict orders not to respond to provocations. Back then, we entertained the illusion that war was still avoidable. That group was ambushed near the city of Slovyansk. Gennady died from gunfire. Six days later, the same Russian officers detained Volodymyr Rybak, a local politician in Horlivka who wanted only to raise the Ukrainian flag in his town. At his side was 19-year-old activist Yuriy Popravko. They tortured them, split open their stomachs and pushed them, while still alive, into the river. That is how Russia's war in Donbas started.
